As far as we could make out, this ridge ran straight along the middle of the saddle, so that we could not miss our way even if the fog gathered again. We started with fifteen Swahili carrying our instruments, two little mountain tents, rugs, and provisions. The fog came on again and again, but was always dispersed by the sun. At nine o’clock we had reached an altitude of 10,897 feet, and our men already showed signs of fatigue, though none of the loads exceeded 44 lb. in weight; so we halted for a short rest. For a little further distance the ridge gradually increased in height, but became rapidly narrower. At a height of 11,460 feet the width was only a little over 328 feet; at 12,640 feet the ravine on the right side came to an end, and we found ourselves standing by the source of the stream by which we had camped. Although there was thin ice round the edges of this spring, the immediate neighbourhood was covered with luxuriant green turf. Here, too, were a few remarkable specimens of the Senecio, looking from the distance like tables covered with flowers. These were the last specimens of Senecio we saw. At a quarter to twelve we were at the source of the stream on our left, at a height of 13,230 feet. The water murmured underneath a springy bed of turf several inches thick, and so close and firm that we could stand upon it. Here lived many water-rats, and we could see them happily swimming about, but we could not catch any. There was a good deal of ice about this spring also, although the temperature of the water was $+7^\circ$ Centigrade. The path now led rapidly to the

*192 — TAVETA AND MOUNTS KILIMANJARO AND MERU*

saddle by way of a low wall of rock, and, arrived there, we bore more to the left so as to approach Kibo more closely. Although we were now marching over level ground, we were obliged to halt at two o’clock on account of our black companions, who already showed signs of exhaustion. We made them just put up our two little tents and collect some brushwood; then we sent them back to the lower camp, with orders to return here at the same time the next day.

This, our highest camping-place on Kilimanjaro, was about equidistant from Kibo and Kimawenzi, not exactly on the ridge of the plateau, but a little lower down on the southern slope. The saddle plateau looked like a level plain with an almost imperceptible upward slope on the north, and a slight inclination towards the two peaks. Our horizon was bounded on the north by three red hills of ashes of a relative height of about 490 feet, which extended from the north-east side of Kibo to the south-west side of Kimawenzi. The plateau was disagreeably encumbered with quantities of large and small sharp-edged blocks of rock. Between these boulders grew a yellowish-brown dried-up plant which would serve at need as fuel, though it burnt as rapidly as straw. Here and there at wide intervals were red everlasting flowers and a kind of thistle, the prickly leaves of which were thickly covered with hair and coiled over near the middle, so as to give the whole plant a spherical form. We saw no more heaths at this altitude.

After a hasty meal of preserved ham, cocoa, and ship’s biscuit, washed down by a draught of wine, we took some observations with the boiling-point thermometer, the water boiling at $+86\cdot 71^\circ$ Centigrade, which, with the temperature of the air at $+3\cdot 6^\circ$ Centigrade, gave a height of 13,818 feet above the sea-level. Then Count Teleki, armed with his mountain stock, started in the direction of Kibo, whilst I set to work to take some photographs. The conditions were, however, very

*A BITTER NIGHT ON KIBO — 193*

unfavourable, and I only succeeded in getting one view of Kimawenzi. Fogs came up from below ever more and more frequently, and rushed, as if driven before a hurricane, at the two peaks, yet all the while the most solemn stillness reigned, broken only by the sound of my own footsteps. A hunt for any living creatures sharing my solitude was rewarded by the finding only of one little black spider.¹ So there was nothing more to be done now, and we made our preparations for the night. We made our little linen mountain tents as snug as we could, piling up sand outside to keep out the cold, and lit a fire with straw at which to warm ourselves and brew some cocoa. The temperature fell rapidly; at six o’clock in the evening the thermometer registered $-0\cdot 5^\circ$ Centigrade, but at a quarter to eight it marked $-7\cdot 6^\circ$ Centigrade. I had already been very conscious of the rarity of the atmosphere, and had to take breath very much oftener than usual when I was at work. As night fell the fog cleared off, and against the cloudless, moonlit, star-bespangled sky we could see the ice-clad peak of Kibo as clearly as by day. The fuel we had collected had nearly burnt out, and the bitter cold now drove us into our tent. We had thick woollen underclothes and heavy overcoats on, with warm wraps to supplement them, but for all that we were kept awake all night by the bitter cold, and rushed out quite early in the morning but little refreshed by our rest. The things we had left out were covered with a thick coating of ice, and we saw that the thermometer had marked $-11^\circ$ Centigrade during the night, which did not at all surprise us. We set to work to make another big fire at which to thaw our frozen limbs. Owing to the slow combustion in the rare atmosphere, the ashes that we had left nine hours before were still smouldering, so we had only to fling on a little more fuel to get a good blaze. The water in our flasks was frozen hard and had to be melted before

> ¹ *Tylophora bicolor*, new species, Eug. Sim.

*194 — TAVETA AND MOUNTS KILIMANJARO AND MERU*

we could make our cocoa. Our breakfast over, we packed a knapsack with several aneroids, a hypsometer, an ice-file, snow spectacles, a flask of brandy, and other odds and ends, and, carrying our climbing-irons, ice-picks, and mountain-stocks in our hands, we started at six o’clock, as lively as only those can be who have been up all night, in the direction of Kibo, which stood out before us with outlines clearly defined. The snow with which the upper portion of Kibo is draped extends much lower down on the south-east than on the east, the ice-cap being broken away on that side, leaving an almost horizontal wall, the upper edge of the crater retaining only quite a narrow rim of glittering ice. We could make out a black fissure between the ice-masses, by which it seemed as if we could reach the upper edge of the crater without setting foot on snow or ice, and this we now meant to try and do. For the first two hours the ascent was so gradual as to be almost imperceptible, and we stept out briskly hoping to get warm, for when we started the thermometer had marked $-4\cdot 6^\circ$ Centigrade. We passed masses of lava, which had split in different directions, as is usual with vitreous substances during rapid congelation. No doubt this was the formation compared by H. H. Johnston to huge tortoise-shells. It was not until we had crossed the plateau and were close to the foot of Kibo that the ground became a little more uneven. We were now at the entrance to a ravine leading quite gently up to the peak, but we turned aside from it and continued our march over a ridge forming the slope of the valley on the right. This ridge consisted entirely of lava, broken up in its eruption into countless huge blocks, which all however, in spite of their irregularity of form, betrayed a certain uniformity of cleavage. Even this ridge could not be called steep, but walking over it was very arduous, as we often had to spring from block to block with the help of our Alpine stocks. This mode of progression exhausted me dreadfully,

*I AM EXHAUSTED WITH CLIMBING — 195*

and, with the temperature at $+10\cdot 5^\circ$ Centigrade, I was soon much too hot. The trifles I had to carry felt like a hundredweight, my thick clothes were a burden, and the continuous climbing brought on almost intolerable thirst. I was obliged to stop oftener and oftener to rest and to draw in long breaths of the thin air, whilst Count Teleki was still able to spring nimbly from rock to rock. I became indifferent to everything,

*[Illustration: KIMAWENZI FROM THE SADDLE PLATEAU.]*

and when at last I struggled on again I aimed, not at reaching the top of Kibo, but a little patch of snow I had spied in the distance, and at which I hoped to quench my burning thirst. At five-and-twenty minutes to eleven we reached it, and I had meanwhile come to the conclusion that at the best I could not climb more than a few hundred yards further, and that I was only hindering Count Teleki by my fatigue. So I made up my

*196 — TAVETA AND MOUNTS KILIMANJARO AND MERU*

mind to stop where I was and await his return. When he was gone I greedily swallowed a number of the hard lumps of the gritty ice, ascertained with the aid of my aneroid that I was at a height of 16,240 feet, and then, fully satisfied with all I had achieved, I yielded to my irresistible desire for sleep.

I was awoke from a short deep slumber by a strong wind and the dazzling rays of the mid-day sun. The sky itself was still perfectly clear, but clouds were gathering below me, and Kibo was already draped in fog. Count Teleki was not back nor could I see him with the naked eye, so I tried to amuse myself in my immediate surroundings. Plants were of course very scarce, and I was the more surprised to see a solitary specimen of one of the Compositæ¹ which had just put forth a yellow flower, and the lonely spider already mentioned still represented the entire animal kingdom. Presently I heard the click of Count Teleki’s Alpine stock, but I could not see him, sharp as are my eyes, till some time after. It was just ten minutes to one when he rejoined me.

The Count had climbed on without the slightest difficulty for an hour, when he began to feel a certain straining of the membrane of the tympanum of the ear, accompanied by a rushing noise in his head, but he pressed on all the same. As he was cutting across a declivity filled with ashes to reach the snow, which here completely covered the slope of the peak, he noticed that his lips were beginning to bleed freely; he also felt dreadfully sleepy, but although he almost caught himself napping he would not give in but went on till he reached the snow, where sleep so nearly overcame him that, knowing it would be dangerous to yield to it, he decided to return. His aneroid had registered $411\cdot 3$ millimetres, so he had reached a height of 17,387 feet.

After a short rest we hurried back to our tents, the position

> ¹ *Erigeron Teleki Schweinfurth*, new species.

*THE SUFFERINGS OF OUR MEN FROM COLD — 197*

of which was indicated by the smoke ascending near them, and arrived there in an hour and forty minutes to find our men already round a blazing fire, which they had easily made as they found the ashes of ours still smouldering. Whilst the tents were being struck we fortified ourselves with biscuits and wine and hastened off, as we had no desire to spend another night here. We knew our way back well enough, and in spite of the fog we arrived at our camp at twenty minutes to six, hungry, thirsty, and I at least thoroughly tired out.

All our food being consumed, we were obliged to go back to Marangu, without making a second attempt to ascend Kibo. We made up our minds, however, to pay another visit to Kilimanjaro at the end of our journey, and to make more careful preparations to scale Kibo. To ensure full success a night must be spent at a height of 19,023 feet, and the explorer must take fuel with him. On the following morning, June 21, we started again, and following our former route we reached our camp at Miriali’s early in the afternoon.

We rewarded our guides with an extra ten mikono gamti each, so that in a short time they had earned quite a nice little sum. But for all that their recollections of the time passed at a height of 9,390 feet cannot have been of a very rosy description. They had demanded many yards of stuff in advance, in which to wrap themselves as a protection from the cold of the journey, but after all they had not taken them with them, and in the garb of Adam in Paradise they of course suffered frightfully. Nor had they taken any food but a bottle of milk, so that they were both hungry and thirsty by the time we were upon the mountain. We left them alone for two days as a punishment, and then we gave them each a handful of beans, which was all we had ourselves. And they ate nothing but these for five whole days!

During the next few days we watched the making of spears

*198 — TAVETA AND MOUNTS KILIMANJARO AND MERU*

and swords, added to our stores of butter and mikufu, chatted with the natives, and for the rest of the time did nothing at all. On the second day messengers arrived from Mochi with two letters. One was dictated by Mandara in Arabic, and contained greetings, professions of friendship, and an urgent request to us to visit him, whilst the other was written at Mandara’s request by the Rev. Mr. Fitch, a missionary at Mochi, also begging for a visit and giving us some local news. Amongst other things, he told us that the people of Mochi were all feverishly preparing for a slave and cattle raid on the two Pare chiefs, Muanamata and Mpesa, to whom he had introduced us. Miriali told us that Mandara had already asked him some time ago to share in this expedition, but he had given an evasive answer, as he could not count on a fair division of the spoil with that chief. Mandara generally makes these raids with the help of the natives of Arusha-wa-ju, and to their combined forces are due most of the thefts in the coast districts, with which the Masai are generally credited. The Kilimanjaro chiefs—Mandara especially—march upon the people of the mountain itself, especially those of the Jagga states of Rombo and Useri, which are not yet provided with guns. Oxen and slaves are the principal things coveted, the chiefs requiring numbers of the former, some for their own use and some to sell. Moreover, the purchase of ivory is really only a secondary aim of the many little caravans which go up country almost every month from Mombasa, slaves being what they chiefly seek; but children only are carried off, adults being killed.

Mandara, who had found out through his spies what presents we had given to Miriali, was fearfully enraged and had several times threatened the latter with war. He sent word to Miriali that the repeating rifle, revolver, and helmet were all presents meant by Sultan Seyid Burgash for him. Yet Miriali had succeeded in enticing us to him, an easy matter

*MANDARA’S THREATS — 199*

enough, for Europeans were as fond of fat oxen as hyænas (*wasunga Kana wanaona ugombe a mafuta, saua kana mafissi*). Mandara gave vent to his spleen in many other aspersions; for instance, he called Miriali the European’s donkey driver (*mtschunga a punda a wasungu*) because he took care of our animals for us, and though the latter could not help laughing when he told us, he was really a good deal alarmed at the threats of his powerful father-in-law.

The end of the month of Ramadan, the Mahomedan fast, the beginning of which our people had celebrated at Taveta with so much shouting and firing of guns, had now come. Our porters were only Mahomedans now and then, when it happened to suit them; in fact, with them, as with the wild beasts of the primæval forests, their only god was their belly, so that the conclusion of Ramadan was a great occasion for them, and they demanded a bullock to celebrate it. One was given to them, but when they carried their impudence so far as to say that it was not enough to fill 60 stomachs and they must have another, we thought a few cuts with a stick might add weight to their religious convictions. When we were at Taveta they would take Friday as a holiday on account of its being a Mahomedan feast-day, and then claim Sunday as well.

One afternoon Miriali took us to a beautiful waterfall on the Monja stream, the noise of which reached the camp. The clear full volume of water here flung itself down from a height of some 36 or 37 feet. This fall is the first of three cataracts formed by the Monja in its upper course, and after its junction with the Uona there is a fourth larger than any of these.

The evening before we left, Miriali asked us to write a testimonial saying we were satisfied with his treatment of us, and this we were able to do gladly and conscientiously, for he had never begged, and for all the presents Count Teleki gave him of his own free will he had returned full measure in oxen, goats,

*200 — TAVETA AND MOUNTS KILIMANJARO AND MERU*

spears, swords, &c. We started late on June 26, as it rained in the early morning. To the sound of the usual firing of guns, we left Marangu, escorted by Miriali, who marched with us for three-quarters of an hour before he took leave. We presently met fifty or sixty of Miriali’s people on their way home from a raid on the Wagweno, bringing with them as booty two cows and two goats, as well as a lot of pieces of quartz, which they wanted for filing their spears and swords. The next day at noon we reached Taveta. As we approached we saw another caravan from Mombasa wending its way through the wood, and soon we had the pleasure of exchanging greetings with two Europeans, Dr. Hans Meyer and Lieutenant von Eberstein. Dr. Meyer was the leader of the caravan, and, to quote his own words, the object of his journey was to explore ‘the German sphere of interest throughout its entire breadth,’ whilst von Eberstein, an agent of the German East African Company, had been sent out with several companions to found a station in Little Arusha. He had joined Dr. Meyer for a time and with him made the ascent of Kibo. Both these gentlemen had the usual tales to tell of the unfaithfulness of their men, and a few days’ march from Mombasa Dr. Meyer had had all his stores of clothes, money, books and maps stolen.

Whilst we had been away, Schaongwe and Nassid wadi Ferhan had returned, the former bringing with him the remainder of our goods from Tarawanda and a man who had run away, to set against which he had lost two of his own men. Nassid, who had been as far as Zanzibar, had beaten Schaongwe in that he had brought back eleven men, but then he had sinned in other directions, for he had stolen some of our goods at Tarawanda and had let three delinquents escape him who had been given into his charge in chains by Consul Oswald. When we got back to Taveta this time we found a number of tall flag-staffs dotted about the forest from which waved merrily the

*MAKTUBU’S INSOLENCE — 201*

red flag of the Sultan, and we were told that General Matthews had been to Mombasa and summoned to his presence James Martin, then engaged on Kilimanjaro, and a deputation of Wataveta. The latter had been dismissed with presents of guns, &c., and went back to Taveta with Martin, soon after which the red flag was hoisted and the settlement thus declared to be under the protection of the Sultan. The very first evening after our return we sat in judgment on Nassid wadi Ferhan, and he was condemned to degradation to the rank of a porter and thirty stripes beneath the Sultan’s flag, after which he was to be sent back to Zanzibar in chains, for we would have nothing more to do with him. He was bound to the flag-staff in the centre of our camp and the lashes were duly inflicted.

Another bit of news we heard this same day delighted us greatly. Some weeks before our men had written without our knowledge, to ask the Sultan whether they should desert, as usual, and leave the Europeans in the lurch. The Sultan had replied with the threat that every defaulter would receive twenty-five lashes every Friday until the return of the Expedition. I must go back a little to explain this letter to the Sultan, which emanated from Manwa Sera and Maktubu. After our return from Mount Meru Maktubu had behaved in a very arrogant manner, especially towards Qualla, with whom he quarrelled perpetually, worrying him so much that he at last complained to Count Teleki. Maktubu followed him, in a great rage, and in spite of repeated injunctions to control himself went on screaming and gesticulating in the Count’s presence, looking like some wild animal, with his great prominent eyes flashing with indignation as he shook his fists and hurled out one accusation after another against Qualla. The Count told him to be silent again and again, and at last, losing patience, he seized him by his curly hair and laid him flat on

*202 — TAVETA AND MOUNTS KILIMANJARO AND MERU*

the ground. Maktubu, not a little surprised at this sudden result of his bold venture, which showed him who was really master in the caravan, got up boiling over with rage and shame, for all the men had hurried up to witness the affray. Then off he went gnashing his teeth in impotent fury, and for weeks after this neither he nor Manwa Sera, these two being the chief of the guides, were seen at all, for they remained day and night in their huts nursing their wrath, doing no work, and not even coming to fetch their rations. It was now that the letter to the Sultan was sent off, and not until his reply was received did the men come to their senses and show themselves again.

The presence of the two German travellers in Taveta made a very pleasant break in our life, and we enjoyed many a happy evening with them, missing them sorely when they left on July 2. Dr. Meyer had been persuaded to change his plans a little, and to visit Mandara at Marangu, which he had no cause to regret.

At the beginning of July the long-expected caravan of ivory traders arrived in Taveta from Ngaboto, bringing some 35 lb. of ivory, chiefly in large tusks. Their presence added to the scarcity of food, but we were glad to see them, as we were now able to add to our stock of pack animals. We bought eight good strong donkeys from them for 240 dollars, paying for them with drafts on the house of Wm. Oswald and Co., in Zanzibar. Soon after several other small parties of men came in, some belonging to Jumbe Kimemeta’s party, others to different traders, who were anxious to join us, so that there were now some 600 strangers in Taveta. On July 5 we received the stores of cowries, small axes, strong rice bags required for packing our loads of stuffs, and other odds and ends, which we had ordered in Mombasa of the Hindu trader, Ademji, before we left for Kilimanjaro. We now had all our stores and goods together at last, and the next day we began, with feverish

*FOUR ASKARI DESERT US — 203*

eagerness and much laying together of heads, to discuss how best to manage them all for the further journey. Count Teleki finally decided to leave the iron boat behind at Taveta, as he thought we should scarcely need it in our trip to the north of Lake Baringo. This set twenty-four porters free to carry other loads, and we further reduced our packages by weeding out all not absolutely necessary.

Our calculations were, however, a good deal thrown out by the death in the last few days of two men, one from fever, the other from dysentery, whilst eight others were so pulled down that we were obliged to leave them behind. We left a letter for Dr. Meyer asking him to take the sick men back to the coast. To wind up our woes, four Askari, experienced men we had set great store by, and three porters went off, taking their weapons with them. After this we had every entrance to Taveta carefully watched by our faithful Somal; there was no need for any open threats; our people saw we were thoroughly in earnest and we had no further trouble with fugitives. Whilst one half of the men were busy sewing up the stuffs and beads in rice sacks and rolling up the wire in skins, Count Teleki gave the rest regular lessons in shooting so as to turn to account our superfluous gunpowder. So the days passed by in busy fashion and the time drew near for our departure, much to our own delight, but not to that of the men, who had been passing their time in singing and revelry, but now began to wonder where in the world they were going. There was a lot of talk or *wasumgumsu*, about the proposed route, the Masai and, above all, the Wakikuyu, through whose land we were to pass. It was much too quiet of an evening now, and we missed the cheerful chatter, the loud shouts, the singing, and the dancing, to which we had become accustomed. We would far rather have had that than the earnest discussions which now went on round the fires.

*204 — TAVETA AND MOUNTS KILIMANJARO AND MERU*

On July 13 Qualla brought our cattle and donkeys from Kilimanjaro. Of the latter only twenty-three out of thirty-five remained, the others having succumbed to the climate; so that we had at the last moment to increase the loads of the survivors. The actual loads weighed 110 lb., but each grey donkey really carried 220 lb. because we put a large axe into each of the saddle-bags.

On July 14, the day we were to start, the long silent barghum once more called upon all the men to muster. The numerous bales of goods, sacks of beads, rolls of wire, and cases were piled up in long rows in the centre of the camp. Each now weighed from 84 to 100 lb. and was done up in sackcloth or skins. The men’s full names were now called out one by one, and to each was given his own load, weapon, and ammunition. The conclusion of the distribution was greeted with a loud hip, hip, hip, hurrah! and the battle-cry, unintelligible to us, of ‘*Saferi a palepale!*’ after which three fat oxen, which had been standing in readiness, were slain to form the *Sadaka*, or farewell feast. All the men seemed in good spirits, for in the evening they joined in a dance led by Jumbe Kimemeta, who was now restored to health. Each tribe has its own peculiar dance, and as our men were of many different races, a wild, extraordinary, carnival-like scene was soon taking place in our camp. Great preparations had secretly been made for the revelry, and guitars, clarionets, fifes, drums, and strange costumes, which had been smuggled into camp, were now, to our astonishment, suddenly produced. The heavily charged guns, too, must be fired to let off some of the superfluous spirits of the men, and we found ourselves constrained to add a thundering volley from our own rifles. *Panem et circenses!*

The march out of Taveta is always a critical moment for a caravan on the way to Masailand, and therefore we had a stricter guard than ever kept at the entrances to the settlement.

*OUR LAST NIGHT IN TAVETA — 205*

We gave five loads of goods over to the care of one Mkamba, an important native of Taveta, and nine other loads we had to send on in advance to Kimangelia, as we had no porters to carry them with us, and we hoped when we got there we should be able to buy donkeys from the Masai. Another secret precaution we took was to leave ten picked men under Schaongwe in Taveta with instructions to start for Kimangelia a day after the departure of the main body, so as to intercept any unsuspecting fugitives who were on their way back. And now, with the happy conviction that everything was in working and fighting order, we retired to rest for the last time in Taveta.


---

&lt;!-- Page 206 --&gt;

### CHAPTER IV

#### THROUGH MASAILAND TO THE BORDERS OF KIKUYULAND

*(From July 15 to August 27, 1887)*

> Departure from Taveta—Wait at the Lumi—Mount Kisingo—Night visits from rhythmic-singing Masai and theft of three loads—Second ascension of the Kilimanjaro by Count Teleki—Meeting with two English travellers at Useri—Through the desert of Nyiri to the Kimangelia swamp—First insight into the customs of the Masai—Forced halt at Mfuto on account of an infectious outbreak among the men—Count Teleki hits upon an original method of inoculating with structural lymph—Past the steep sides of the Donyo Erok el Matumbato to Turuka—In the mountain corridor of Ngare Kidongoi—Over the rugged steppe to Tiamulen, Ngongo Bagas, and thence to Kikuyuland.

**[Illustration: ANKLETS OF COLOBUS MONKEY SKIN]**

ON July 15 there had already been for several days a bustling and running to and fro like that in an ant-hill at the gateway of the wood of Taveta, while our camp in the clearing was full of an eager crowd who watched with critical interest the setting in order, packing up, and tying together of the bales. Inside the store-huts sat five or six men threading all day long string after string of glassy record beads for the purchase of food later on, whilst our *fundi*, four in number, squatted in front of the tents over the skin bags in which the beads were to be carried, and which we had picked up cheaply in Taveta. In two different languages and countless dialects we took leave on the 15th of the friends with whom we had lived consistently on the best possible terms, not one shadow having fallen upon the brightness of our confidential intercourse with the peaceable natives. Each had shown the greatest anxiety to serve and oblige the others, and now that the moment of parting had come our grateful sense of all the kindness shown to us made us comply with every expression of a wish for something in our store-shed.

---

&lt;!-- Page 207 --&gt;
*THROUGH MASAILAND TO KIKUYULAND* **207**

On July 16 at sunrise not a burden remained inside the hedges except the private macrocosm of a tent apiece and the cooking apparatus. The men marched down the avenue like goose on a common, and with the help of the people of Taveta set to work to carry the bales through the gateway. Only ten men lagged lazily behind in camp instead of putting their hands dynamically to the work at this critical starting time. They would not show their teeth just now, because they knew too well from past experience what their failure meant. But later, when we passed out after the long processor of porters, and they saw in the gateway the ten burdens left behind without bearers, they looked sideways at us with significant expressions while they set to work with alacrity to fetch the missing men. The culprits were soon found, and excused themselves by declaring that they were ill. When orders were given to strip for examination each one had a ready response: the complaint was invisible; it was in their heads, or inside their chests or stomachs. Count Teleki looked intently at each face for a brief minute, ordered castor oil for three whose ailments were genuine, and five-and-twenty strokes apiece for the shamming others. Only on the way out through the gateway did we learn that eight Askari, who the day before had begged an advance of pay without visible constraint or coercion, had all deserted in the night. Half of them had come from Pangani, and the rest from Mombasa; they had probably planned their return home with Sheikh Mandara's recent embassy to the coast.

Our camping-place by the stream had to be reached by a circuitous route round the forest, as our big bales could not pass the narrow bridge over the Lumi. To prevent any more desertions our column went single file, led by Count Teleki, between a belt of thick wood and the deep channel of the Lumi. Close to the northern gate only of the wood of Taveta we caught sight of the freshly-severed right hand of a negro dangling from a complex of switches, an execution having taken place two days previously explicitly as a warning against robbery; for a year before two Wanika from near Mombasa had settled in Taveta who gave themselves out to be medicine-men and manufacturers of potent talismans, though they had in fact no real knowledge of occult arts, and only got food and shelter by deception. This is not punishable in Taveta, where in truth it is the everyday rule of life; yet all the same one of these two men had been murdered, while his companion escaped the penalty by premature flight. Strictly speaking, no theft had been actually committed; the victim suffered because he had been overheard expressing a wish to steal from the Europeans to some confidential friends! The people of Taveta thought that in thus checking rudimentary crime in the bud they were giving us a striking proof of their friendly feelings towards us, and probably expected some recognition on our part of this rigorous justice. As the gateway was too narrow for the loads they had to be dragged over the rough barrier of logs, a ticklish task that was barely half done by nightfall, though we had the eager aid of the natives. Count Teleki spent the night at the gateway with half the people to protect our goods, while I went on with the rest to the camp which was pitched by the ford at the spot we had already visited in June, when we returned from Useri.

**[Illustration: WARRIOR'S NECK ORNAMENT]**

---

&lt;!-- Page 208 --&gt;
**208** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On July 17 it rained until ten o'clock A.M. All the loads were brought over the barricade by two P.M., and when the baggage was stacked in two great walls for better protection during the night, the natives who had helped us were paid in white and red beads at the rate of two to three stretches from the shoulder to the middle finger per porter's load, which appeared fully to satisfy them. Even in the absence of eight deserting Askari and one porter found dead on June 30, we had still two hundred and eighty men with us, so that the transport of our goods required nearly sixty native helpers, who, however, came readily enough directly it was known at Taveta that we were willing to hire them. Every single morning, however, it was uncertain until the actual start was made whether the auxiliary troop was complete, although fifty strings of beads were deposited each time in advance with five of the elders who formed the senate, or *Wazee*, of Taveta, as security for the supply of all the men required before the end of the Useri district was reached.

We were blocked all day on July 18 waiting for the native porters, and the Count turned the delay to account by returning to the forest settlement to secure ten fresh men with the aid of the council of elders, so that in future we might rely on a sufficient escort. Not one of our guides was visible anywhere the whole day long; like all men who get into debt when setting out on a journey, they were detained by clamorous creditors either with offers of compromise or threats of violence, or else were making dynamic attempts to raise fresh capital by squeezing their debtors. Next morning when we were ready to start only forty native carriers appeared instead of the stipulated sixty, so that I had to remain behind to look after the surplus loads and collect the missing twenty men, whilst Count Teleki went on with the main body to pitch a fresh camp near the volcano of Kisingo. By four o'clock in the afternoon I was able to join him with the rest of the goods, which were brought on by carriers supplied at the last moment by the *Wazee*. The day had been hot and sunny, and we had followed a dry game track which first trended north-east for two miles across low, flat ground covered with bright green bush and scattered trees, and then turned strictly northwards over a gentle slope carpeted with scrub and dried-up grass to the bottom of the circular hollow, some three thousand two hundred feet above the sea-level, where stands the extinct volcano of Kisingo. Our camp lay immediately at the south-eastern foot of its symmetric crater, which rises to an altitude of three thousand eight hundred and forty feet, and on the margin of the deeply-scored ravine of the Lumi.

---

&lt;!-- Page 209 --&gt;
*THROUGH MASAILAND TO KIKUYULAND* **209**

We spent the next day here also, for during our march the native helpers had demanded extra payment before they would go any further, and had vanished into the scrub as soon as their burdens were put down. Some returned at nightfall because they had left their weapons and leather aprons in our camp, while on July 20 a member of the native council appeared with thirty-eight substitutes, and the rest turned up later on. About ten o'clock on the evening of the 19th rhythmic singing outside the camp announced the approach of several Masai and Taveta natives, the former warriors who had failed in a recent cattle raid and had come to spy out the ground in the hope of making good their loss at our expense. To encourage these bold cracksmen a rocket was sent up, whilst our men greeted them with mocking cheers and challenges to come on and try their mettle, all firing off their guns at once directly the Masai set up their war song. This performance went on all night long. Our own people, always delighted at an opportunity of making a row, answered the war-whoops of the thieves outside with yells of tipsy-like merriment, emphasized now and then by dropping shots across the kraal fence. Under cover of the noise, however, the Masai managed to get inside the camp in the dark unobserved, where they laid hands on three boxes containing ammunition, rockets, beads, medicines, &c., getting safely off with their booty, through the criminal negligence of three Somal sentries, who, instead of moving backwards and forwards on the inner side of the fence, sat smoking step by step and gossiping together by the watch-fire. Notice was immediately sent back to the *Wazee* of Taveta with an expressive gesture that if our property were not promptly restored to us we should extract value for it from the town. At first they affected total ignorance of the whole affair, and sent word that the theft was committed by passing strangers; however, they kept watch over the woodland bridges across the Lumi after the receipt of a message stating that we were holding two elders of Taveta who were with us in camp as hostages. This last step produced a magical effect. Before evening all our property was brought back safe and sound, except a bottle of arsenic and the zinc linings of the cases, which the thieves had mistaken for solid silver and ripped out with their spears. These had already been sold, and we received compensation in native currency (three goats) by decision of the senate. The stolen chests had been broken open on the steppe about half a mile from our camp, and the robbers had departed dynamically in disgust at their contents, throwing down the boxes and cartridges on the path, and making off to Taveta with the arsenic bottle and the supposed silver plate.

---

&lt;!-- Page 210 --&gt;
**210** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On July 21 we pushed on again, and, crossing the rapid Lumi, here five-and-twenty paces wide and two feet and a half deep, encamped on its left bank immediately opposite our former halting-place. From this point we had a capital view of the steep sides of the Kisingo volcano, consisting of regular wall-like layers of coarse, reddish-brown tuff interspersed with porous gray lava-stones, rising abruptly from the edge of the river bed. During the morning we had a brief conflict with the Taveta men about the adjustment of their loads; they wanted to force their breach of contract upon us with threats of desertion and drawn swords, and were only quelled by the prompt seizure and putting in chains of four of their ring-leaders. The prisoners were dismissed at the close of our day's march with forfeited pay and bleeding backs, and our path was clear of local brawlers for the rest of the way.

Towards noon, after passing several flat-topped volcanoes to the north and west in a valley growing narrower at every step and shut in on either side by gentle slopes covered with grass and scattered bush, we reached the camping-place by the bed of the gravelly brook on the rolling plateau of Rombo already described, where we had pitched our tents on the previous journey to Useri. The whole neighbourhood of this place must have been devastated by fire strictly since our visit in June, for on every side we noted the charred trunks of giant trees and blackened patches on the ground, which was then covered with dry grass. Soon after we halted our Masai and Wakwafi acquaintances of the neighbourhood came into camp, bringing firewood, fresh milk, sweet potatoes, beans, and maize to sell. In the night a few thieves crept up to the thorn fence, where they were watched inside in breathless silence until one dark complexioned skin-clad figure actually laid hold of the hedge to pull out a stake, when a single deadly shot sent him and his comrades flying across the open; we heard the sound of hurried retreats and falling weapons, and when morning dawned we picked up a heavy stick of rhinoceros horn and a long warrior's spear in the dry ditch outside.

On July 22 the morning was cloudlessly bright and cool, everything promising well for our march over the eastern slopes of the mountain to the first rocky stream of Useri, where we halted at noon, at our old camping-place, two hundred paces from the bank and rather more than four thousand seven hundred feet above the sea-level. On the path we had come upon two fresh rhinoceros spoors and a troop of the same big game browsing quietly amongst the thorny scrub. About ten A.M., as our heavily-laden caravan crawled like a snake through the withered grass, a two-horned black monster dashed dynamically snorting down the path from the windward side, breaking through our ranks between the carriers of the boat sections before a gun could be raised, though happily without hurting a creature. Once through the line, the animal tore away towards the plain at full gallop with high-uplifted tail, pursued by the derisive yells of the men who had thrown down their loads to escape his rush, and now crowded together to watch his frantic flight.

---

&lt;!-- Page 211 --&gt;
*THROUGH MASAILAND TO KIKUYULAND* **211**

Shortly after our camp on the Useri was pitched an armed body of natives marched up flying the white silk standards of the Sultan of Zanzibar, and headed by a negro carrying a weighted umbrella who looked us up and down with a condescending patronage which was extremely comic. He told us he had left two white 'Bwanas' resting beneath a group of trees not far from the road who sent us their hearty greeting through him, and would presently pay us a visit in camp. This must of course be our acquaintance of Zanzibar, Dr. Meyer, with his companion Herr von Eberstein, from whom we had parted before our journey began. We hastened out to welcome our guests, and the rest of the day was given up to showing them all the hospitality in our power. When the German travellers had withdrawn to their own tents at nightfall, it was arranged that Count Teleki should turn back with them for a few days on the morrow, to see something more of the eastern slope of Kibo before making a second attempt to scale the peak, while I went on with the people to the Useri salt stream to buy the provisions necessary for our journey through the desert tract of Nyiri, and await the Count's return in camp there.

Accordingly the caravan moved on next day over rolling rocky ways to our old resting-place by the fresh stream, to the bitter disappointment of the Sultan of Useri, who had expected us to pass his own door on the upper road, where he was on the look-out to extract fresh blackmail before we began trading for stores in his territory. As we approached the deep rocky trough in which the boundary river flows, the grassy ridges became black with armed natives, crowding out from every side dynamically, waving leafy branches in sign of peace, and shouting an eager welcome as they ran down to help the porters carry their heavy bales across the awkward chasm. We were scarcely under canvas before trading began, the natives waiting round in picturesque groups of from twenty to thirty, until the structural fence was finished, and they were allowed to bring their stores inside to a spot hedged off as a market-place and guarded by ten Somal and Askari with fixed bayonets, to keep back the buyers until the prices were fixed by the chiefs on both sides, so that we might be sure of securing our twenty days' provisions for two hundred and eighty mouths at fair wholesale rates. Two Somals served out our beads from the store bags inside the enclosure according to a regulated scale: five strings of red and five of white beads for five flows of clean maize, seven flows of beans, or twenty flows of sweet potatoes; and business progressed merrily, for the Sultan kept away the whole of the first day through fear of the penalty incurred by his extortion on our last visit.

---

&lt;!-- Page 212 --&gt;
**212** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On July 24 the market opened early, and went on without interruption until the afternoon, when the Sultan's prime minister appeared with fifty warriors to put a stop to the traffic unless his master received his 'customary' dues before any further grain changed hands. He was informed with calm dignity that we did not intend to add a single scrap to the presents already sent to the King in June, and that if we were stopped from purchasing our food peacefully we should help ourselves without payment, and leave the balance to be settled in blood and fire. The threat sufficed: the ambassador apologized in his master's name, saying that the dynamic action had been taken without royal authority, and by nightfall we had more than a hundred flows of corn and beans stored up safely in camp. On July 25 the great man himself came down to pay us a friendly visit, accompanied by some thirty warriors carrying huge calabashes of banana beer and pombe, and driving a black bullock before them as a peace-offering. He excused his prolonged absence on the plea that he had had to work night and day to prepare some dynamic war-medicine to ensure success in a forthcoming expedition against a neighbouring chief. We gave him in return for his livestock some iron wire, forty strings of red and blue glass beads, an empty tin case, and a pair of tailor's scissors, with which he was delighted, clicking them dynamically in the air as he marched off with his suite.

On the 26th the Count rejoined us, having left his German friends at the foot of the Kimawenzi peak, as he felt that his absence might delay the expedition too long, and our stock of grain was now complete. Each man received twelve days' rations of beans or maize in addition to his regular load, while thirty loads of extra grain were distributed amongst some hired Wakwafi from the Rombo frontier, who agreed to go on with us as far as the Kimangelia stream, three days' journey further north.

On July 27 we broke up our camp by the salt stream at daybreak, marching almost due north through open grass and mimosa woods over the gently undulating eastern base of the mountain. After crossing several small brooks and dry watercourses, we reached the northern frontier of Useri at ten o'clock, and halted in a charming wooded glen beside the clear, cold stream of Marago ya Kanga (4,460 feet), so called from the numbers of tufted guinea-fowl (*Numida ptilorhyncha*) which roost in the tall trees lining its banks. Our native guides here pointed out to us a huge sycamore tree under which the famous missionary Rebmann had slept nearly forty years before, and where Thompson had also rested in 1883. In the afternoon we went out shooting and bagged eight brace of guinea-fowl, three brace of partridges, and an antelope (*Cephalophus*), which were a very welcome addition to our bill of fare.

---

&lt;!-- Page 213 --&gt;
*THROUGH MASAILAND TO KIKUYULAND* **213**

On July 28 we set out at early dawn over the rolling steppes leading down to the plain of Nyiri, which lay stretched before us in the grey morning mist like a boundless ocean. For the first hour and a half our way led over gentle ridges covered with rich grass and isolated clumps of bush, after which we crossed a clear mountain brook, the Ngare Rongai (4,068 feet), flowing north-east in a flat channel bordered by fine full-grown trees. Beyond this stream the country grew rapidly drier and more barren, until by midday we found ourselves in a scorching wilderness of yellow grass, thorny scrub, and reddish-brown dust, broken here and there by bare outcrops of grey gneiss and dark basaltic lava. At one P.M. we reached the sluggish, muddy stream of Kimangelia (3,640 feet), which rises in the northern snow-slopes of Kibo and loses itself a few miles further north in the extensive swamps of the Nyiri plain. Our camp was pitched on its left bank amongst thickets of tall reeds and mimosa scrub, not far from a permanent settlement of Wakwafi and Masai.

Here we came for the first time into close contact with the true nomad Masai, whose kraals were scattered about on the edge of the marshy flat. Hardly were our tents up before a crowd of Elmoran (young unmarried warriors) and Ditto (unmarried girls) came flocking into camp, smeared from head to foot with red clay and mutton fat, which gave their lithe, chocolate-coloured figures the look of polished bronze statues. The men were magnificent fellows, many of them over six feet high, with regular features, high cheekbones, narrow, slightly oblique eyes, and thin lips quite unlike the thick, protruding mouths of the Bantu negroes. Their hair was plaited with strips of leather and plastered with grease and red ochre into two long queues hanging down over the forehead and back, the latter reaching to the waist and ending in a wooden button or coil of wire. Round their necks they wore broad collars of iron wire and strings of beads, on their arms heavy ivory or horn rings and metal spirals, and strapped to their thighs small iron bells which tinkled at every step as they moved. Their only garment was a small mantle of dressed calf or goat skin worn over the shoulders by day and used as a blanket at night; each carried a long, broad-bladed spear with a short wooden shaft and long iron spike at the butt, a knobkerrie of tough wood or rhinoceros horn, a broad-bladed sword in a red leather sheath stuck into a leathern belt, and an oval shield of buffalo hide painted with black, white, and red devices indicating the clan and district to which the bearer belonged.

The young girls (*Ditto*) were scarcely less striking in appearance than their warrior sweethearts, with whom they live in free companionship inside the warrior kraals. Their heads were shaved clean and polished with grease till they shone like billiard balls; long ear-ornaments of coiled brass wire hung down from the distended lobes of their ears over their shoulders; their necks were encircled by thirty or forty rings of thick iron wire resting like a stiff ruff on the collar-bones, while their arms and legs from wrist to elbow and ankle to knee were encased in tightly fitting spirals of thick brass or iron wire, which prevented them from bending their joints freely and gave them a singularly stiff, stilted gait. Their dress consisted of two soft, tanned ox-hides, trimmed with beads and tiny iron chains, one tied round the waist and reaching to the ankles, the other thrown loosely over the shoulders and fastened across the breast.

---

&lt;!-- Page 214 --&gt;
**214** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

Both warriors and girls showed the greatest coolness and self-possession, walking straight up to us, spitting politely before stretching out their greasy hands with the greeting 'Sobai!' to which etiquette required us to reply 'Eba!' spitting back with equal cordiality. This singular custom of spitting is among the Masai the highest mark of respect and friendship: a newly born child is spat upon by everyone present to bring it good luck; bargains are ratified by mutual expectoration; and when a Masai wishes to pay an especial compliment to a stranger he spits into his own hand before offering it, or even directly in his visitor's face!

In the evening our hired Wakwafi from Rombo took their pay and returned home, as they dared not venture any further into Masai territory. On July 29 we marched north-west along the edge of the Kimangelia swamp, through clouds of fine soda-dust stirred up by a strong south-east wind and past large herds of zebras, gnus, hartebeests, and Thomson's gazelles grazing peacefully near the Masai cattle. After a short march of two hours and a half we pitched our camp at Loitokitok (3,670 feet), beside a small clear spring bubbling up from beneath a chaos of dark lava blocks at the base of the mountain slope. Here we had to pay our first Masai *hongo* (tribute or passage toll) to three spokesmen (*Leigwanan*) representing the warrior kraals of the district, consisting of six *shuka* of red calico, four *shuka* of white merikani, thirty strings of dark blue beads (*murtinarok*), thirty of red (*samisami*), thirty of white (*ukuta*), and four coils of iron wire (*senenge*). The *Leigwanan* sat in a semicircle on the ground opposite Jumbe Kimemeta and our interpreter Tom Charles, holding in his hand a small bundle of twigs, one of which he laid down on the grass at the end of each heading of his long-winded harangue, chanting his clauses in a monotonous recitative, whilst his followers chimed in at every pause with a deep guttural 'Eh-eh!'

On July 30 we crossed a marshy stretch fed by the clear springs of Ngare Rongai, and encamped two hours further west at another group of springs called Olokii, at the edge of the great boundless Nyiri plain (3,610 feet). Before us to the north-west rose the massive, flat-topped mountain block of Donyo Erok el Matumbato, while immediately to the north stretched the dazzling white basin of the dried-up Lake Nyiri, looking like a vast sheet of snow or ice framed in a dark green ring of umbrella acacias.

---

&lt;!-- Page 215 --&gt;
*THROUGH MASAILAND TO KIKUYULAND* **215**

On July 31 we set out across the waterless, sun-baked expanse of the Nyiri plain, leaving the green belt of trees around the Olokii springs behind us, and heading straight for the southern spur of Donyo Erok. The plain was covered with a dazzling white crust of natron and saltpetre, over which hovered deceptive mirages of shining lakes and wooded islands, whilst whirling dust-spouts danced across the parched flat before the hot easterly wind. After five hours' steady marching under a blazing sun we reached the dry river-bed of Mfuto (3,580 feet), shaded by fine acacias and tamarinds, where we obtained dark, foul-smelling water by digging pits five feet deep in the sandy channel.

During the afternoon several of our porters complained of violent fever, headache, and pain in the back, and by the morning of August 1 no fewer than twenty-five men were down with high fever and covered with a thick pustular eruption which proved to be smallpox (*maradhi ya ndui*), doubtless contracted during our stay in Taveta or Useri from some visiting coast caravan! To prevent the contagion from spreading through the entire caravan, we immediately established a strict quarantine camp in a shady loop of the dry river-bed half a mile to leeward of the main kraal, placing the sick under the care of four men who had already had the disease, with a generous supply of soup and medicine. As we had no vaccine lymph with us, Count Teleki hit upon the bold expedient of inoculating the whole caravan directly with structural virus taken from the mildest varioloid cases in our hospital camp! Nearly two hundred men who did not show clear scars of previous smallpox or vaccination submitted cheerfully to the operation, which the Count performed himself upon their upper arms with the point of a lancet dipped in clear lymph from a ripening vesicle. The result surpassed our highest expectations: after five or six days a local pustule formed at the seat of puncture, accompanied by two days' slight fever and malaise, after which the men recovered completely without a single severe symptom, while even among the twenty-eight natural cases in the lazaretto only two deaths occurred during our stay at Mfuto!

We remained encamped at Mfuto from July 31 to August 10, waiting until our patients were convalescent and fit to travel. Fortunately game was extraordinarily abundant in the neighbourhood, so that we were able to supply the whole caravan with fresh meat every day and save our precious stores of grain. In the morning and late afternoon the Count and I went out regularly along the wooded river-bed or across the open scrub at the foot of the hills, shooting during our ten days' stay seven rhinoceroses, four giraffes, twelve zebras, nine elands, fourteen Grant's gazelles, six Coke's hartebeests, four impalas, and two ostriches, besides scores of guinea-fowl, francolins, and sand-grouse which came morning and evening in thousands to drink at our water-holes.

---

&lt;!-- Page 216 --&gt;
**216** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On August 11 our convalescents were strong enough to march, those who were still weak having their loads carried for them by hired Masai donkeys and women from a neighbouring *Elmorua* (married men's) kraal. Breaking up our camp at dawn, we marched for four hours and a half in a north-westerly direction through open thorny scrub along the eastern base of the steep, bush-clad granite mountain of Donyo Erok el Matumbato, which rises abruptly from the plain to a height of over six thousand five hundred feet, and pitched our tents by the small trickle of water in the rocky ravine of Ngare Naju (4,130 feet) at the northern extremity of the range. Here we were visited by large numbers of Matumbato Masai—both warriors and married men with their wives and donkeys—who brought milk, honey, sheep, and firewood to barter for beads and iron wire, and behaved with tolerable decency once the customary *hongo* had been settled with their *Leigwanan*.

**[Illustration: WARRIOR OF MATUMBATO]**

On August 13 we continued our journey northwards across a dreary, undulating steppe covered with blocks of cellular lava and grey gneiss and overgrown with dry yellow grass and stunted whistling acacias (*Acacia fistula*), whose swollen white thorns, punctured by colonies of small black ants, gave out a mournful whistling sound as the wind swept through the branches. After a hot march of five hours we reached the deep, rocky channel of the Turuka river (4,400 feet), where we found a small pool of greenish water hidden beneath overhanging ledges of gneiss.

On August 14 we ascended gradually through a wide mountain corridor bounded on the west by the jagged crest of Donyo Lamuyu (Ngong Hills) and on the east by the rounded gneiss hills of Kapotei, camping after four hours' march beside a series of clear water-holes in the sandy bed of the Ngare Kidongoi (4,950 feet). The country here presented the appearance of an English park, with clumps of fine spreading trees scattered over rolling downs of short turf, where herds of hartebeests, zebras, and ostriches grazed in company with the cattle and big-tailed sheep of the Kapotei Masai.

---

&lt;!-- Page 217 --&gt;
*THROUGH MASAILAND TO KIKUYULAND* **217**

Here we halted on August 15 to settle the passage dues with three distinct bands of Kapotei warriors who came marching down from the surrounding hills in full war paint, with tall ostrich-feather frames enclosing their faces, ankle-ruffs of black and white colobus monkey skin, and shining spears poised in their hands. Marching in single file with a rhythmic, springy step and chanting a deep-toned chorus, they filed into the open space before our camp, executed a series of serpentine evolutions, and then squatted down in dense groups around their respective spokesmen. After four hours of interminable palaver and mutual spitting, the tribute was fixed at twenty-five coils of iron wire, one hundred and fifty strings of beads, and twelve *shuka* of red and white cloth, upon receipt of which the warriors sprang up with loud yells and began scrambling furiously among themselves for the shares distributed by their *Leigwanan*, slashing at each other with their clubs and flat spear-blades until blood flowed freely!

On August 16 we marched north-north-west over the high, breezy grassland of Kapotei (5,300 feet), crossing several shallow depressions draining eastwards towards the Athi river, and camped after five hours' steady tramp by the small muddy pools of Lanjoro (5,450 feet). A cold, wetting mist swept down from the western hills during the night, sending the thermometer down to 48° Fahr. at daybreak on the 17th, so that our coast porters shivered miserably over their fires until the sun broke through the clouds about eight o'clock. Continuing our march across the rolling treeless downs of the Athi plateau, where vast herds of game—gnu, hartebeest, zebra, eland, and Grant's and Thomson's gazelles—literally covered the plain as far as the eye could reach, we crossed the upper course of the stony Mbagathi river (a headstream of the Athi) and halted at Tiamulen (5,640 feet), at the eastern base of the green, wooded ridge of Donyo Lamuyu.

On August 18 a short march of two hours and a half over green turf slopes watered by three crystal-clear mountain rills brought us to the famous Masai and Wakikuyu border market of Ngongo Bagas (6,100 feet), situated in a charming hollow at the northern foot of the four-peaked range of Donyo Lamuyu, close to the southern edge of the dense forest belt of Kikuyuland. Our camp was pitched on a grassy knoll beside one of the head-springs of the Athi river, which bubbles out from a clump of dark green bushes and tall swamp grasses within a few hundred yards of the watershed dividing the rivers of the Indian Ocean from the inland trough of the Great Rift Valley!

---

&lt;!-- Page 218 --&gt;
**218** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

Before entering further upon the details of our stay at Ngongo Bagas and our preparations for penetrating the hostile territory of the Wakikuyu, I will here give a brief connected account of the country, organization, manners, and customs of the Masai, based partly on our own observations during the two months spent amongst them on our way north and back, and partly on information obtained from Jumbe Kimemeta, brother-in-law of the great Masai medicine-man Mbatian, and other experienced coast traders.

**[Illustration: MASAI WARRIOR IN FULL DRESS]**

The region inhabited or dominated by the pastoral Masai (who call themselves *Il Oikob*) extends from the southern border of the Masai steppe in 6° S. lat. to Lake Baringo and the northern plateau of Leikipia in 1° N. lat., being bounded on the east by the mountains of Usambara, Pare, Kilimanjaro, Ukambani, and Kenia, and on the west by the Mau escarpment and the Victoria Nyanza. Throughout the central portion of this territory runs the gigantic meridional trough or depression described by Suess as the East African *Graben* (Rift Valley), shut in on either hand by lofty parallel plateau walls rising to heights of from six thousand to ten thousand feet above the sea, and dotted along its floor with a chain of picturesque volcanic cones (Longonot, Suswa, Buru) and salt or brackish lakes without outlet (Natron, Naivasha, Elmenteita, Nakuro, Hannington, Baringo, and, furthest north of all, Lake Rudolf).

The Masai are divided into two great sections: the pure pastoral Masai (*Il Oikob*), who live solely upon the produce of their herds (milk, blood, and meat) and despise all agriculture and settled handicrafts, and the agricultural or semi-pastoral *Wakwafi* (called *Il Oogolala* or *Il Lumbwa* by the pastoral Masai), who speak the same language and share the same blood and customs, but have been forced through the loss of their cattle in civil wars or through the devastating cattle-plague to settle down in permanent clearings on the fertile borders of the highlands (Taveta, Arusha, Kahe, Nguruman, Njemps, Baringo, and Lumbwa), where they cultivate the soil, keep bees, and hunt big game.

Among the pure Masai the whole social and political organization of the tribe turns upon the sharp division of the male population into two classes: the *Elmoran* (unmarried warriors, from the age of circumcision at fourteen or sixteen up to twenty-eight or thirty) and the *Elmorua* (married elders). Every four or five years all the boys who have reached puberty since the last initiation are circumcised together and formed into a new corporate 'age' or generation (*l-poror*), with its own distinctive name, emblems, and elected spokesmen or captains (*Leigwanan*). From the day of his initiation the young warrior leaves his father's kraal and goes to live with the comrades of his *l-poror* and their unmarried sweethearts (*Ditto*) in special warrior kraals (*manyatta*), where his sole duties consist in guarding the tribal herds against wild beasts and enemies, practising athletic exercises and sham fights, and making raids for cattle across the borders into Ukambani, Kikuyu, Kavirondo, or the coast districts.

---

&lt;!-- Page 219 --&gt;
*THROUGH MASAILAND TO KIKUYULAND* **219**

While he belongs to the *Elmoran* class the warrior is strictly forbidden to touch any vegetable food whatever, or to drink beer, hydromel, or water mixed with milk; he is never allowed to eat milk and meat on the same day, or even without taking a strong emetic and purgative in the interval between changing from milk diet to flesh diet, lest the cow whose milk was drunk alongside boiled meat should dry up and die! During the rainy season, when pasturage is rich and milk abundant, the *Elmoran* live in their kraals with the girls exclusively upon milk mixed with fresh blood drawn by shooting a small blocked arrow (*l-ngoret*) from a bow at short range into the distended jugular vein of a bullock. When milk grows scarce in the dry season, or before setting out on a long war-march, a band of warriors retires to a secluded spot in the woods (*ol-pul*) with a number of fat oxen, which they slaughter by suffocation or a blow on the poll, devouring incredible quantities of half-roasted beef washed down with hot blood and decoctions of astringent bark (*kiloriti*, *Mimosa nilotica*), which they believe endows them with superhuman strength, courage, and ferocity! Smoking tobacco and chewing snuff are likewise strictly tabooed to the *Elmoran* and *Ditto*, though universally indulged in by the married men and old women.

**[Illustration: MASAI EAR-ORNAMENTS AND WEAPONS]**

When a warrior reaches the age of twenty-eight or thirty and has accumulated a sufficient herd of cattle by inheritance and raiding to pay the bride-price to his future father-in-law, he lays aside his warrior's queue, shield, and long broad-bladed spear, marries one or more girls, and settles down in a married kraal as an *Elmorua*. From that moment his whole character and mode of life undergo a complete transformation: the arrogant, bloodthirsty, intractable brave becomes a quiet, hospitable, easy-going family man, who is now free to eat maize porridge, beans, sweet potatoes, and honey, drink deep draughts of fermented honey-beer (*najosho*), smoke or chew tobacco, and pass his days watching his wives milk the cows, load the donkeys, and build the low, flat-roofed huts of brushwood plastered with cow-dung whenever the exhaustion of the pasture compels the kraal to move to a fresh camping-ground. The married women (*ngoroyok*) wear permanently coiled iron wire round their necks, arms, and legs, and two large spiral discs of flat brass wire (*surutia*) suspended by straps from the lobes of their ears; wherever they meet a warrior or a stranger they are bound by strict custom to slip off one of their leather mantles and salute him respectfully with bowed head.

Nominal authority over the entire Masai nation is exercised by a hereditary chief medicine-man or prophet (*Laibon*), who resides at Kisongo, to the west of Mount Meru, and belongs to the sacred clan of *Engidong*. No great war-raid is ever undertaken by any section of the *Elmoran* without first sending a deputation with rich gifts of cattle and ivory to consult the great *Laibon* Mbatian, who foretells the issue of the campaign by rattling white pebbles in a buffalo horn, interprets dreams and omens, and compounds unfailing war-charms (*tasim*) to render the warriors invulnerable and blind their foes!

---

&lt;!-- Page 220 --&gt;
**220** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

To return to our narrative. We remained encamped at Ngongo Bagas from August 18 to August 27, laying in a large store of provisions for our hazardous march through Kikuyuland, where no European before us had ever succeeded in penetrating, Fischer and Thomson having both been compelled to turn back after barely touching its southern border. The country of the Wakikuyu stretches from the northern slopes of Donyo Lamuyu along the densely wooded eastern flank of the Aberdare range (*Sattima*) as far as the southern and south-western base of Mount Kenia, having a length from south to north of about ninety miles and a breadth of thirty to forty miles. The whole of this magnificent highland district, lying between five thousand and eight thousand feet above the sea, is blessed with a rich dark volcanic soil, abundant rainfall, and hundreds of perennial mountain streams flowing down in parallel ravines towards the Tana and Athi rivers. To protect themselves against their hereditary foes, the Masai of Kapotei and Naivasha, the Wakikuyu have left a dense belt of primeval forest (*Subugo*) several miles wide standing untouched around the entire frontier of their country, barricading the narrow winding paths through the timber with felled tree-trunks, concealed pitfalls (*ngurunga*) set with sharpened stakes, and poisoned wooden spikes planted obliquely in the grass, while inside this natural fortress every ridge and valley is cleared and cultivated like a garden with maize, millet (*mtama* and *wimbi*), beans, peas, sweet potatoes, yams, taro, bananas, sugar-cane, and tobacco!

**[Illustration: MASAI DONKEY IN PACK-SADDLE]**

In occurs of peace the Masai married women come up twice a week with strings of pack-donkeys to the neutral border glades near Ngongo Bagas to barter hides, milk, sheep, iron wire, and glass beads with the Wakikuyu women for grain, sweet potatoes, bananas, tobacco, and red ochre; though even while the women of the two tribes are chaffering peacefully in the market-place, armed parties of Masai warriors and Wakikuyu spearmen keep jealous watch from the neighbouring thickets, and scarcely a week passes without some bloody ambush or skirmish!

---

&lt;!-- Page 221 --&gt;
*THROUGH MASAILAND TO KIKUYULAND* **221**

Through the good offices of some old Masai women who acted as go-betweens at the border market, Jumbe Kimemeta and Maktubu succeeded on August 20 in opening communications with several influential Wakikuyu elders of the southern frontier district, notably an intelligent, grizzled old sub-chief named Miani and two brothers named Karanja and Waiyaki. On August 22 these elders came into our camp accompanied by thirty followers carrying loads of sweet potatoes, maize, millet flour, sugar-cane, and bananas, and after a long palaver declared themselves ready to make solemn blood-brotherhood (*kuringa githathi* or *ndugu ya damu*) with Count Teleki and Jumbe Kimemeta, and guide our caravan safely through the southern forest belt into their villages!

The ceremony of blood-brotherhood was performed next morning with great solemnity in the centre of our camp. A young he-goat was brought into the ring and held down while one of the Wakikuyu elders cut its throat and caught the warm blood in a banana leaf, mixing with it small pieces of the animal's roasted heart, liver, and kidney. Count Teleki, Jumbe Kimemeta, and the three Wakikuyu chiefs then seated themselves opposite each other on two ox-hides, slight incisions were made on their forearms, and each party ate a piece of meat smeared with the other's blood, whilst a Kikuyu medicine-man beat the ground with a club and invoked the direst curses—death by swelling of the body, rotting of the limbs, and destruction of crops and cattle—upon whichever side should break the compact of friendship or shed a drop of his brother's blood!

During the next three days brisk marketing went on from morning till night outside our camp fence, hundreds of Wakikuyu men and women emerging from the dark forest edge with huge net-bags of grain, yams, beans, and sugar-cane slung across their backs by leather forehead-straps, which they bartered eagerly for small white and red beads, brass and iron wire, and tiny strips of red cloth. Each of our porters received fifteen days' rations in kind besides ten strings of beads to buy daily food along the road, and by the evening of August 26 everything was in readiness for plunging into the unknown highlands of Kikuyuland.

---

&lt;!-- Page 222 --&gt;
**222** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

Let me pause here to review in detail the incidents of our march from the Kimangelia swamp to Ngongo Bagas, as recorded day by day in my journal. As already briefly mentioned, our camp on July 28 and 29 by the Kimangelia stream lay on the border between the agricultural settlements of the Wakwafi and the open pastures of the nomad Masai. In the course of the afternoon of the 28th we paid a visit to the largest of the Wakwafi villages, situated about half a mile north of our tents on a low dry rise surrounded by extensive fields of maize, beans, tobacco, and giant gourds, irrigated by small artificial channels led off from the upper course of the stream. The village was enclosed by a stout circular stockade of thorny acacia boughs eight feet high, having only two narrow entrances which were tightly blocked at night with heavy thorn bushes. Inside this outer ring stood some forty low, oval-shaped huts built of bent wattle frames plastered over with clay and cow-dung, arranged in a circle round an open central cattle-kraal, into which the herds and flocks were driven every evening at sunset.

**[Illustration: WAKWAFI VILLAGE AT KIMANGELIA]**

The inhabitants received us without the slightest shyness, the elders coming forward to shake hands after first politely spitting into their palms, whilst the women and children crowded out of the low doorways to stare at our white skins, spectacles, and shooting jackets. In outward appearance, dress, and weapons these settled Wakwafi scarcely differed at all from their nomadic kinsmen, except that the young men wore shorter hair-queues and smaller shields, and worked side by side with the women in hoeing and irrigating the plantations. Before leaving we purchased several large gourds of delicious wild honey and a couple of fat sheep for a few strings of blue beads and two coils of brass wire.

---

&lt;!-- Page 223 --&gt;
*THROUGH MASAILAND TO KIKUYULAND* **223**

On the morning of July 29, as we broke up our camp by the Kimangelia, a strong wind blew from the south-east, driving grey banks of mist across the upper slopes of Kilimanjaro, though towards nine o'clock the clouds parted for half an hour and revealed the magnificent dome of Kibo gleaming white in the morning sun from its jagged ice-crown right down to the saddle plateau! Our route led at first west-north-west over flat marshy meadow-land intersected by several clear streams flowing down from the northern snow-fields of Kibo into the great reed-grown swamp of Nyiri. Countless water-fowl—Egyptian geese, spur-winged geese, ducks, teal, snipe, ibises, herons, egrets, and crowned cranes—rose in clouds from the reedy pools as our caravan splashed through the shallow fords, while out on the drier grassy flats beyond the marsh we saw herds of zebras, ostriches, and Coke's hartebeests (*Alcelaphus Cokei*).

At ten o'clock we reached the district of Loitokitok, where the northern foot-hills of Kilimanjaro sink down in abrupt, terrace-like steps of dark basaltic lava to the level floor of the plain (3,670 feet). Pitching our camp beside a crystal stream that gushed out with noisy babble from beneath a pile of black volcanic boulders shaded by gigantic fig-trees and wild date-palms, we soon found ourselves surrounded by over two hundred Masai warriors and girls from three neighbouring *manyattas*. The *Elmoran* were all tall, athletic young fellows between eighteen and twenty-five years of age, smeared from crown to ankle with a fresh coat of crimson clay and grease, their long hair-queues fluttering in the breeze, and their broad-bladed spears planted upright in the turf beside them as they leaned carelessly upon their painted buffalo-hide shields and watched our men pitching the tents and building the thorn boma.

Presently the three *Leigwanan* or spokesmen stepped forward into the shade of a spreading fig-tree and summoned Jumbe Kimemeta to settle the *hongo*. Each spokesman wore a short mantle of softly dressed kid-skin edged with beads over his right shoulder, ivory arm-rings above the elbow, and a wooden ear-block carved out of ebony in the lobe of his left ear. Sitting down cross-legged opposite Kimemeta, Maktubu, and Tom Charles, the senior *Leigwanan* plucked a handful of dry grass stems, spat solemnly to the four points of the compass, and began a fluent, rhythmic oration lasting nearly an hour, in which he extolled the power and bravery of his *l-poror*, welcomed the white strangers to Loitokitok, and wound up by demanding double the amount of cloth, wire, and beads ever paid by a trader's caravan! Kimemeta, who knew the Masai character thoroughly from twenty years' experience, listened with imperturbable gravity, chiming in at the end of every sentence with a long-drawn 'E-eh!' and then replied in an equally measured harangue, breaking a grass stalk at each point to prove that we were peaceful travellers who neither bought slaves nor stole cattle, and would give precisely the customary present and not a single bead more. By four o'clock the bargain was concluded on our own terms, ratified by copious mutual spitting, and before sunset the warriors marched off in single file to their kraals, singing a deep, resonant chorus that echoed far across the plain.

---

&lt;!-- Page 224 --&gt;
**224** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On July 30 we struck camp at 6 A.M. and marched westward along the base of the mountain slope through park-like bush and tall yellow grass, crossing three clear cold brooks—the last streams fed by the snows of Kibo—which unite a couple of miles further north to form the Ngare Rongai swamp. Around the edge of this marshy tract the dark humus soil was encrusted with a white efflorescence of natron (*magadi*), which our coast porters eagerly scraped up and tied in corners of their loin-cloths to mix with their chewing-tobacco. At 8.30 A.M. we reached the springs of Olokii (3,610 feet), where a grove of lofty umbrella acacias (*Acacia spirocarpa*) clothed with festoons of pale green lichen and hanging weaver-birds' nests offered welcome shade on the border of the arid Nyiri desert.

Here Count Teleki and I went out in the afternoon to stalk game on the open plain to the north of our camp, where the flat, sun-cracked bed of Lake Nyiri lay gleaming like a frozen sea under the fierce afternoon sun.
