During the rainy season the waters pouring down from the northern face of Kilimanjaro and the surrounding hills collect in this shallow depression without outlet, forming a sheet of brackish water some twelve miles long by five wide; in July and August, however, the northern and central parts of the basin were completely dry and covered with a dazzling white crust of carbonate of soda beneath which lay treacherous black mud, whilst only at the southern end around the mouths of the Kimangelia and Ngare Rongai streams remained a reedy lagoon swarming with hippopotami, crocodiles, and water-fowl. Walking cautiously through the scattered mimosa scrub bordering the flat, the Count bowled over a fine male Grant's gazelle (*Gazella Granti*) with graceful lyre-shaped horns measuring twenty-six inches in length, and half an hour later brought down two fat zebras, which thirty of our men speedily cut up and carried into camp.

**[Illustration: HEAD OF GRANT'S GAZELLE]**

---

&lt;!-- Page 225 --&gt;
*THROUGH MASAILAND TO KIKUYULAND* **225**

Before dawn on July 31 the water-calabashes were all filled at the Olokii springs, as a long waterless march lay before us across the western arm of the Nyiri desert to the dry riverbed of Mfuto. Starting at 5.30 A.M., we steered due north-west straight towards the bold blue mass of Donyo Erok el Matumbato, which rose like a steep rocky island from the level sea of the steppe. For the first two hours our path led over grey dusty soil thinly sprinkled with tufts of wiry grass and low thorny caper-bushes; then we entered the completely bare bottom of the ancient lake-bed, where the horses' hoofs broke at every step through a crackling crust of salt and natron into loose alkaline dust that rose in suffocating clouds as the column of nearly three hundred men tramped along under the scorching glare. As the sun mounted higher strange mirages played continually around the horizon: lines of flat-topped acacias seemed to stand reflected in clear blue water, and a troop of ostriches trotting across the plain half a mile away looked like gigantic long-necked water-monsters wading through a shallow lagoon!

Towards ten o'clock the ground began to rise slightly and became strewn with blocks of grey gneiss and reddish quartz, amongst which grew dry scrub, fleshy euphorbias, and aloes with bright coral-red flower-spikes. At 10.45 A.M. we reached the green belt of tall acacias and tamarinds lining the dry sandy channel of the Mfuto river (3,580 feet), where our vanguard set to work with spades and pointed sticks to dig water-holes in a bend of the bed. At a depth of four to five feet a dark, slightly brackish fluid oozed slowly through the sand, smelling strongly of sulphuretted hydrogen, though after being boiled and filtered with a pinch of alum it served passably for making tea and cooking porridge.

As already stated, it was here at Mfuto on the afternoon of July 31 and the morning of August 1 that smallpox broke out violently among our porters, compelling us to remain stationary for ten full days in this dreary wilderness until the epidemic had been stamped out by strict isolation of the sick and Count Teleki's bold and successful inoculation of the healthy men with humanized lymph! Let me extract from my diary the daily record of our life and hunting adventures during those ten anxious days in the quarantine camp at Mfuto.

---

&lt;!-- Page 226 --&gt;
**226** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

*August 1.*—In the morning Count Teleki examined every man in the caravan, sent five-and-twenty unmistakable smallpox patients down to the isolation camp established in a shady bend of the river-bed half a mile to leeward under charge of Qualla and four immune attendants, and inoculated eighty of the healthy porters with clear lymph taken from a mild case. In the afternoon the Count went out towards the southern foothills of Donyo Erok to procure meat for the people, and returned at dusk after shooting two rhinoceroses, an eland, and a zebra! One of the rhinoceroses, an old bull with a front horn twenty-four inches long, charged furiously through the thick thorn scrub at the smoke of the first shot, and dropped dead to a solid eight-bore bullet in the chest within six paces of the Count's feet!

*August 2.*—Three fresh cases broke out during the night and were removed to the lazaretto, while the remaining hundred and twenty men were inoculated before breakfast. Towards nine o'clock I set out with four men along the dry bed of the Mfuto towards the north-east, where the river-channel spread out into a wide grassy flat dotted with umbrella acacias and huge red ant-hills ten to twelve feet high. Scarcely a mile from camp I sighted a herd of fifteen giraffes browsing quietly on the tender topmost shoots of a clump of mimosa trees, their long necks towering above the flat green crowns of the thorns, while their dappled chestnut-and-yellow hides blended so wonderfully with the checkered sunlight and shadow among the branches that at two hundred yards' distance it was almost impossible to distinguish their bodies from the tree-trunks! Creeping up under cover of a tall ant-hill to within a hundred and thirty paces of the nearest animal, a magnificent old bull standing fully eighteen feet high, I fired at his shoulder with my .500 Express rifle; at the report the whole troop swung round and went off in a clumsy, rocking gallop, their long tails switching rhythmically and their necks swaying like ship-masts in a heavy swell, though the wounded bull lagged behind after three hundred yards and soon came to a standstill under a dead tree, where a second bullet behind the shoulder brought him crashing to the ground. Fifty porters fetched in every scrap of the meat, skin, and marrow-bones before afternoon; the meat of the giraffe, especially that of a young animal or cow, tastes very like good veal, while the long glutinous tongue and the marrow from the huge leg-bones made a capital dish for our own table.

**[Illustration: GIRAFFES IN THE MFUTO SCRUB]**

---

&lt;!-- Page 227 --&gt;
*THROUGH MASAILAND TO KIKUYULAND* **227**

*August 3.*—One of the severest confluent smallpox patients died early this morning in the hospital camp, and was buried deeply in the sand covered with heavy boulders and thorn-bushes to keep off the hyenas, which prowl round both camps in scores every night, uttering their hideous laughing howl. In the afternoon Count Teleki bagged a third rhinoceros, two Coke's hartebeests, and three Grant's gazelles on the open plain to the west.

*August 4.*—No new natural cases have appeared since the 2nd, and the inoculated punctures on the men's arms are beginning to take finely, showing small red papules surrounded by a slight halo. Morning minimum temperature 56° Fahr.; afternoon maximum in the shade 89.5° Fahr., with a dry, boisterous south-east wind that fills plates, books, and clothing with red grit. Went out at 3 P.M. with Schaedler and shot two zebras, an impala buck (*Aepyceros melampus*), and nine brace of large yellow-necked francolins (*Pternistes nudicollis*).

*August 5 and 6.*—Most of the inoculated men are now going through the regular reaction fever, sitting shivering wrapped in their blankets around the camp fires with slight headache and swollen axillary glands, though all remain in capital spirits and devour huge rations of boiled giraffe, eland, and rhinoceros meat! On the 5th the Count shot two fine bull elands (*Oreas Canna*) and a fourth rhinoceros at the foot of the hills; and on the 6th I stalked and killed a female giraffe and two Thomson's gazelles (*Gazella Thomsoni*) four miles north of the river.

*August 7 and 8.*—A second patient died in the quarantine camp on the morning of the 7th, but all the remaining twenty-six sufferers are now over the worst stage, their pustules drying up rapidly under daily applications of palm-oil and carbolized vaseline, while the inoculated men have completely recovered from their two days' fever without a single bad symptom! On the afternoon of the 8th a roving party of forty Matumbato *Elmoran* on the war-path against the Wakamba of Kikumbuliu suddenly appeared on the opposite bank of the river-bed, halted in astonishment at the sight of our large stockaded camp, and after hearing from Tom Charles that we had smallpox among the men, bolted upstream as fast as their legs could carry them, spitting furiously to avert the dreaded infection!

---

&lt;!-- Page 228 --&gt;
**228** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

*August 9 and 10.*—Spent both days overhauling and repacking our diminished loads, cleaning instruments, and hunting along the base of Donyo Erok to lay in a three days' supply of dried meat (*charqui*) for the march across the barren Matumbato steppe. During these two days Count Teleki shot three more rhinoceroses (making seven in all at Mfuto!), two giraffes, and four zebras, so that long festoons of meat strips hung drying in the sun and wind on ropes stretched from tree to tree all round our camp. Before sunset on the 10th a small trading party of ten married Masai women and four old *Elmorua* with fifteen donkeys arrived at our lower water-holes on their way home to Ngare Naju from Useri, and willingly agreed for ten strings of beads apiece to let their donkeys carry the loads of our twenty-six convalescents on the morrow.

On the morning of August 11 we gladly turned our backs upon our melancholy halting-place at Mfuto, the convalescent patients marching in a separate detachment a quarter of a mile in the rear of the caravan so as not to come into close contact with the Masai at the next camping-ground. Our course lay north-north-west along the steep eastern flank of Donyo Erok el Matumbato ('the Black Mountain of Matumbato'), a magnificent isolated massif of dark gneiss and granite rising abruptly nearly three thousand feet above the surrounding plain to an absolute altitude of 6,560 feet. The lower slopes were clothed with a dense, almost impenetrable jungle of thorny acacias, euphorbias, sanseviera, and aloes, cut up by deep dry torrent-beds choked with water-worn boulders, while the upper precipices and crags were crowned with dark green forest watered by the heavy mists and clouds which gather almost daily around the summit.

After four hours and a half of brisk marching across the stony talus at the base of the mountain, we rounded its northern shoulder and pitched our camp by the mouth of a wild, picturesque gorge, down which trickled the small clear brook of Ngare Naju (4,130 feet), losing itself in the dry sand within a hundred paces of the cliff foot.

---

&lt;!-- Page 229 --&gt;
*THROUGH MASAILAND TO KIKUYULAND* **229**

Hardly was our thorn hedge dragged into place before crowds of Matumbato Masai came flocking down from half a dozen kraals hidden in the hollows at the foot of the mountain: first groups of old married men and women driving pack-donkeys laden with leather milk-gourds, bundles of firewood, and small fat-tailed sheep, and shortly afterwards a band of eighty *Elmoran* in full gala attire accompanied by forty or fifty *Ditto*. Here we had an excellent opportunity of studying the domestic implements and trade customs of the married Masai. Their milk-vessels (*kibuyu*) are made of long, narrow gourds measuring from two to three feet in length, neatly covered on the outside with cow-hide and provided with a leather cap and carrying-strap decorated with cowrie shells and glass beads. These gourds are never washed out with water—which according to Masai belief would ruin the cows' udders—but are cleaned with cow's urine and smoked over a tiny fire of aromatic wood-chips (*ol-oirien*, wild olive), which imparts to Masai milk a strong, smoky, turf-like flavour to which a European palate only becomes reconciled by sharp hunger!

We remained in camp at Ngare Naju over August 12, as the *Elmoran* spokesmen made protracted difficulties over the *hongo* and tried every conceivable trick to extort extra coils of iron wire and red cloth from us. At midday, whilst the palaver was at its height outside the camp gate, two daring young warriors tried to snatch a bundle of beads from the hands of one of our Somal guards inside the marketing space; in an instant the Somal brought the butt of his rifle down with a resounding whack upon the thief's greased skull, felling him like an ox, whilst fifty of his comrades leaped up brandishing their long spears and yelling their war-cry! Count Teleki and I immediately stepped to the front with ten armed Somal and Askari with cocked rifles, while Jumbe Kimemeta threw his white turban down on the ground between the two parties—the recognized Masai sign for a truce and parley. Seeing our resolute front and the levelled gun-barrels, which the Masai dread above everything, the *Leigwanan* promptly beat back the hot-headed youths with their long ebony clubs, apologized for the attempted theft, which they laid to the stupidity of a raw country lad, and within half an hour accepted our original offer of fifteen coils of wire, eighty strings of beads, and eight *shuka* of cloth in full payment of all demands!

---

&lt;!-- Page 230 --&gt;
**230** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On August 13 we left Ngare Naju at daybreak and struck northwards across an undulating, waterless steppe connecting the northern spur of Donyo Erok with the broken hill-country of Kapotei. The ground consisted of red ferruginous clay strewn with fragments of white quartz, grey gneiss, and porous lava, and overgrown with coarse dry grass and low forests of whistling thorn (*Acacia fistula*), whose black, gall-like excrescences at the base of the long white spines were tenanted by myriads of vicious little ants that rained down upon our heads and necks whenever a porter's load brushed against the boughs. To the left of our route rose several isolated conical hills of gneiss—Leme Boti, Merueshi, and DonyoKisali—while far behind us in the clear morning air towered the twin snow-peaks of Kilimanjaro, eighty miles away to the south-east!

After five hours' hot marching we descended into the broad, steep-sided rocky bed of the Turuka river (4,400 feet), which drains the southern hills of Kapotei westward towards the Great Rift Valley, though at this dry season of the year its boulder-strewn channel contained only a single pool of stagnant, pea-green water sheltered beneath an overhanging cliff of stratified gneiss. Around this solitary drinking-place the sand and rocks were trampled by thousands of game tracks—rhinoceros, buffalo, zebra, eland, and antelope—and covered with millions of bright-coloured butterflies which rose like a cloud of painted petals as our thirsty men rushed down to fill their calabashes.

**[Illustration: WHISTLING ACACIA WITH ANT-GALLS]**

On August 14 our path wound steadily upwards towards the north-north-west through a charming highland valley or mountain corridor shut in on the east by the rounded, grassy hills of Kapotei and on the west by the rugged, picturesque crest of Donyo Lamuyu (the Ngong range). Every mile as we ascended the vegetation became greener and richer; fine park-like clumps of wild olive, juniper, and flat-topped acacias dotted the grassy slopes, and after four hours' march we pitched our camp at an altitude of 4,950 feet beside a chain of crystal-clear water-holes in the sandy bed of the Ngare Kidongoi, where we remained over August 15 to settle the passage tribute with the warriors of Kapotei, as already described.

---

&lt;!-- Page 231 --&gt;
*THROUGH MASAILAND TO KIKUYULAND* **231**

Let me add a few further details regarding the dress and battle array of the three bands of Kapotei *Elmoran* who visited our camp at Ngare Kidongoi on August 15. When equipping himself for war or for a ceremonial parade at the arrival of a large caravan, the Masai warrior fixes around his face a huge oval frame of bent wood thickly set with black ostrich feathers (*en-gopiro*), measuring nearly two feet and a half in height by eighteen inches in breadth, so that only his gleaming eyes, red-ochred nose, and chin show through the central opening! Over his shoulders floats a cape of hawk and vulture feathers or a mantle of scarlet cloth bought from coast traders; round his waist is strapped his red leather sword-belt holding his broad-bladed *sime* and knobkerrie; below his knees are bound broad bands of long black-and-white colobus monkey fur that stream out behind like wings as he leaps into the air; while on his thigh hangs the curved iron war-bell (*ol-twala*), whose deep clang keeps time with the rhythmic stamp of his feet and the guttural chant of his war-song.

**[Illustration: KAPOTEI WARRIOR IN OSTRICH-FEATHER HEAD-DRESS]**

Nothing could be more picturesque or imposing than the approach of these three companies of eighty to a hundred warriors apiece as they wound down the green hill-slopes into the valley of the Ngare Kidongoi, their five-foot spear-blades flashing in the brilliant sunshine, their black ostrich-plume halos nodding above their painted shields, and their deep-chested chorus swelling and dying away on the breeze. When within two hundred yards of our camp each band halted, formed into a dense phalanx with locked shields, and then came charging forward at a run to the gate of our boma, stopping dead at a wave of the *Leigwanan's* club and springing one after another three feet straight into the air without bending their knees, while their long back hair-queues flew up and down and their thigh-bells jangled in wild unison!

After the official *hongo* had been handed over and fought for among the turbulent braves, the girls (*Ditto*) who had accompanied their sweethearts from the warrior kraals came into camp and sang and danced for a couple of hours before our tents, standing in a long row with their hands resting on one another's shoulders and jerking their bodies rhythmically up and down from the hips while their dozens of iron neck-rings and ear-coils clanked an accompaniment to their shrill, monotonous song.

---

&lt;!-- Page 232 --&gt;
**232** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On August 16 we left the valley of the Ngare Kidongoi and climbed the green slope to the north, reaching within an hour the gently rolling summit of the high Kapotei plateau (5,300 to 5,600 feet), which stretches eastward as a vast treeless sea of turf towards the distant blue range of Ukambani (Donyo Sabuk and the Machakos hills), while to the west it rises towards the steep dark ridge of Donyo Lamuyu. Never in all our African wanderings had we beheld such an astounding profusion of wild animal life as here on the breezy Athi plains! As far as the eye could reach across the waving yellow grass, the steppe was literally speckled with dark herds of brindled gnu (*Connochaetes taurinus*), Coke's hartebeest, Burchell's zebra, eland, and Grant's and Thomson's gazelles, grazing peacefully in mixed companies of hundreds and thousands together, and scarcely troubling to trot out of rifle-shot as our long line of nearly three hundred men filed across the plain! Out on the open turf where not a bush or stone offered cover, stalking was by no means easy, for the old sentinel bulls among the gnu and hartebeest stood watching our movements from the tops of the deserted ant-hills, and whenever we separated from the caravan line they gave a loud warning snort, switched their long tails, and set the whole plain in motion at a lumbering gallop; nevertheless, by firing steadily at ranges of two hundred to two hundred and fifty yards, Count Teleki and I bagged during the day's march three gnus, four hartebeests, two zebras, and three gazelles, providing a royal feast for our heavily laden porters when we pitched camp at 11.30 A.M. beside the rain-pools of Lanjoro (5,450 feet).

As there was not a stick of firewood to be found for miles around on this treeless plateau, our men had to cook their meat over fires of dried zebra- and gnu-dung mingled with dry grass-roots. Towards evening a cold driving mist rolled down from the summit of Donyo Lamuyu, drenching everything with moisture and chilling our lightly clad Zanzibaris to the bone.

---

&lt;!-- Page 233 --&gt;
*THROUGH MASAILAND TO KIKUYULAND* **233**

On August 17 we marched for four hours and a half across the northern continuation of the Athi plains, crossing several rocky gullies watered by clear streams that flow down from the eastern face of Donyo Lamuyu to unite farther east in the Mbagathi or upper Athi river. Along the marshy margins of these streams we flushed numbers of large dark snipe and yellow-billed wild ducks, while on the intervening ridges we came twice within close range of rhinoceroses sleeping in the tall grass, one of which Count Teleki bowled over with a single eight-bore bullet behind the ear. At eleven o'clock we pitched our tents at Tiamulen (5,640 feet), on a grassy terrace overlooking a deep wooded ravine near the northern peak of the Lamuyu range.

On August 18 a cool, pleasant march of two hours and a half along the green, well-watered foot-hills of Donyo Lamuyu brought us at 9 A.M. to our long-desired goal, the frontier camping-ground of Ngongo Bagas (6,100 feet), where we set to work immediately to build a strong fortified boma of heavy tree-trunks and thorn-bushes on a gentle slope beside the clear spring-head of the Athi river.

Let me now supplement the brief outline already given of our ten days' stay at Ngongo Bagas (August 18 to 27) with a fuller account of the romantic surroundings of this spot and our daily negotiations with the Masai and Wakikuyu. Immediately to the south of our camp rose the four bold, grassy peaks of Donyo Lamuyu, reaching an elevation of nearly seven thousand five hundred feet above the sea, their eastern slopes clothed with patches of dark forest and rich pasture, while their western face plunges down in tremendous rocky precipices two thousand feet sheer into the barren, volcanic trough of the Great Rift Valley! Half an hour's walk westward from our tents over a low grassy saddle brought us to the edge of this stupendous cliff, whence we looked down upon a wild, desolate landscape of extinct volcanic cones, dark lava streams, and grey bush-steppe stretching away to the opposite wall of the Mau escarpment, forty miles to the west, with the regular, truncated cone of Mount Suswa rising from the floor of the valley to the north-west.

---

&lt;!-- Page 234 --&gt;
**234** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

To the north and north-east of our camp, separated from the slopes of Donyo Lamuyu only by the narrow grassy glade in which bubbled the head-springs of the Athi, stretched the dark, impenetrable wall of the primeval forest of Kikuyu (*Subugo*), composed of gigantic junipers (*Juniperus procera*), podocarpus, wild olives, fig-trees, and tree-heaths festooned with long grey beards of *Usnea* moss and bound together by a tangled undergrowth of brambles, stinging nettles, passion-flowers, and creeping bamboos.

During the first two days after our arrival at Ngongo Bagas (August 18 and 19) we were besieged from dawn till dusk by four or five hundred Kapotei and Matapatu Masai warriors from the big kraals on the southern slopes of Donyo Lamuyu, who demanded exorbitant *hongo* and tried by every means in their power to dissuade us from entering Kikuyuland, declaring that the Wakikuyu were treacherous forest demons who would ambush our column in the dark forest paths, shoot our porters down from behind the trees with poisoned arrows, and massacre every soul in the caravan! Their real motive, of course, was simply trade jealousy: they wanted us to spend all our wire, cloth, and beads in their own kraals or go straight on through the Rift Valley to Naivasha, instead of enriching their hated neighbours and rivals, the Wakikuyu, with our coveted coast goods!

Finding that their bloodcurdling stories made no impression upon us, and that twenty picked Somal and Askari mounted guard day and night with loaded rifles round our thorn stockade, the *Elmoran* gradually quieted down after receiving a handsome present of wire, cloth, and beads on the afternoon of the 19th, and allowed the old Masai women from the neighbouring *Elmorua* kraals to visit our camp freely with milk, honey, and sheep. Through two of these old dames, who spoke the Kikuyu language fluently and went every few days into the forest clearings to buy grain and sweet potatoes, Jumbe Kimemeta sent a friendly message with a few strings of fine blue and white beads to the frontier Kikuyu elders Miani, Karanja, and Waiyaki, inviting them to come down under safe-conduct to our camp and talk over terms for our peaceful passage through their country.

---

&lt;!-- Page 235 --&gt;
*THROUGH MASAILAND TO KIKUYULAND* **235**

On the afternoon of August 20 five Wakikuyu scouts emerged cautiously from the edge of the forest half a mile north of our camp, armed with bows, poisoned arrows, leaf-shaped spears, and long narrow shields painted in red, white, and black zigzag patterns. Kimemeta and Maktubu walked out unarmed to meet them, sat down with them in the grass for an hour's friendly chat, and sent them back delighted with small presents of red cloth and brass wire to fetch their chiefs.

On August 21 about thirty Wakikuyu men and women came down to the edge of the glade with loads of sweet potatoes, maize-cobs, beans, sugar-cane, and ripe bananas, which our men bought eagerly within half an hour; and at noon on August 22 the three influential elders Miani, Karanja, and Waiyaki themselves arrived in camp at the head of forty warriors and women bearing presents of four fat sheep, two jars of sugar-cane beer (*uhi*), and twenty baskets of flour and yams!

**[Illustration: WAKIKUYU WARRIORS AND WOMEN AT NGONGO BAGAS]**

In stature and build these Wakikuyu were far inferior to the tall, lithe Masai, being of medium height (five feet four inches to five feet seven inches), with broad, muscular shoulders, sturdy legs developed by constant hill-climbing, dark reddish-brown or chocolate complexions, and typical Bantu features, though many of the younger men and girls had remarkably bright, intelligent faces and pleasant smiles. Like the Masai, whose dress and ornaments they copy in many respects, they distend the lobes of their ears to an enormous size, inserting round blocks of wood, coils of brass wire, or whole handfuls of wooden skewers five inches long, while the upper cartilage of the ear is pierced with a row of smaller holes holding little rings of iron and copper beads. Their hair is either shaved off except a small tuft on the crown, or twisted into hundreds of tiny ringlets plastered with red clay and grease; round their necks, wrists, and ankles they wear collars and bracelets of iron and brass wire and strings of small white and blue beads, while their sole garment consists of a single dressed goat- or sheep-skin worn over the shoulders with the hair turned inwards.

---

&lt;!-- Page 236 --&gt;
**236** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

As already related, on the morning of August 23 Count Teleki and Jumbe Kimemeta concluded a solemn compact of blood-brotherhood (*ndugu ya damu*) with Miani, Karanja, and Waiyaki, after which the chiefs promised that if we kept strictly together on the march, paid fairly in beads and wire for all food brought into market, and prevented our porters from plundering the plantations or molesting the women, they would guide us safely from district to district right across southern and central Kikuyuland!

From August 23 to 26 our camp glade presented the appearance of a bustling country fair. Every morning by seven o'clock three or four hundred Wakikuyu men, women, and lads came filing out of the narrow forest pathway, bowed nearly double beneath enormous loads of maize, millet (*wimbi*), beans, peas, sweet potatoes, yams, taro (*colocasia*), bananas, and bundles of sugar-cane, carried in wide-meshed bags of string network suspended by a broad leather band passing across the forehead. To prevent any disputes or pilfering on the part of our Zanzibaris, a circular market-space fifty paces across was fenced off with thorn boughs just outside the camp gate under the supervision of Schaedler, Maktubu, and ten armed guards, where food was bought wholesale at fixed rates—one string of small white beads (*ukuta*) or red beads (*samisami*) purchasing three flows of maize or beans, ten pounds of sweet potatoes, or a large bunch of bananas!

While the women were busy trading in the market-place, Count Teleki and I made daily excursions along the slopes of Donyo Lamuyu or down the banks of the Athi head-streams, shooting during our stay at Ngongo Bagas three rhinoceroses, two bushbucks (*Tragelaphus sylvaticus*), four waterbucks (*Cobus ellipsiprymnus*), six impalas, and numbers of wild ducks, geese, and guinea-fowl. On the afternoon of August 26 our three Kikuyu blood-brothers Miani, Karanja, and Waiyaki slept inside our camp with ten of their warriors so as to lead the column into the forest at daybreak on the morrow; every porter had his fifteen days' grain rations tied firmly on top of his sixty-pound bale, and we looked forward with keen excitement to the untried adventures awaiting us behind the dark forest curtain of Kikuyuland!

---

&lt;!-- Page 286 --&gt;

### CHAPTER V

#### TO KENIA

*(From August 27 to October 8, 1887)*

> Entry into Kikuyuland—Friendly reception at the frontier—Description of the country and its inhabitants—The system of *hongo* and ceremonies of peace—Constant misunderstandings and petty conflicts—First violent battle near Getura—Storming of several villages—Passage of the boundary river Maragua—Reception in Kornu—Difficult marches across deep mountain ravines—Peace concluded with the elders—Exit from Kikuyuland—In the border steppes on the south-west of Mount Kenia—Arrival at Ndoro at the foot of Kenia.

**[Illustration: KIKUYU WAR-HORN AND IRON BELLS]**

ON August 27, 1887, at six o'clock in the morning, our bugle sounded the general advance, and preceded by our Kikuyu blood-brothers Miani, Karanja, and Waiyaki, with Jumbe Kimemeta and Count Teleki at the head of the column, our long file of two hundred and eighty heavily-laden men plunged into the narrow, gloomy pathway leading through the primeval frontier forest (*Subugo*) of Kikuyuland. For the first hour and a half we had to scramble in single file along a sunken cattle-track choked with tough creepers, fallen tree-trunks, and slippery mud-holes, and barred every few hundred yards by low artificial stockades of felled timber through which narrow openings scarcely two feet wide had been cut, so that the boat sections, iron wire loads, and ammunition boxes had to be hoisted over one by one with infinite labour. Every now and then our guides warned us to step carefully round deep, narrow pitfalls (*ngurunga*) dug right across the path and artfully covered over with thin twigs, withered fern-fronds, and dead leaves, at the bottom of which were planted upright stakes of hard fire-sharpened wood smeared with virulent arrow-poison (*muriju*)!

---

&lt;!-- Page 287 --&gt;
*TO KENIA* **287**

At eight o'clock we emerged from the dark, dripping belt of virgin forest into a rolling highland country of extraordinary fertility and beauty (6,300 feet), intersected from west to east by countless narrow, flat-topped ridges separated from each other by steep-sided valleys two hundred to four hundred feet deep, at the bottom of each of which brawled a crystal-clear mountain brook shaded by tree-ferns, wild bananas, and overarching thickets of bush. Every single ridge and hillside, except the marshy bottoms and the strips of dense coppice left standing around the homesteads as defensive hedges, was cultivated like a garden from top to bottom with thriving fields of maize, *mtama* (sorghum), *wimbi* (*Eleusine coracana*), beans, peas, sweet potatoes, yams, taro, sugar-cane, and tobacco, fenced in with neat hedges of thorny solanum and scented wild sage!

**[Illustration: FIRST CAMP IN KIKUYULAND (MIANI'S VILLAGE)]**

As our caravan wound along the crest of the first cultivated ridge, hundreds of natives—men, women, and children—came running out from the hidden homesteads on either hand, crowding along the hedges beside the path to stare in breathless wonder at the first white men and the first horses they had ever beheld! Whenever Count Teleki or I rode forward on our grey ponies to keep the line closed up, a chorus of shrill cries of astonishment ('*U-u-u-ui! Ngai!*') rose from the crowd, the women and children tumbling head over heels into the sweet-potato patches in comic terror at the sight of the strange four-footed monsters, while the warriors stood balanced on one leg leaning on their long spears and shouting friendly greetings ('*Mirema! Mirema!*') to our guides.

At 10.30 A.M., after crossing three steep-sided ravines bridged at the bottom by slippery single tree-trunks, we reached the district of our blood-brother Miani (6,250 feet), and pitched our camp on an open grassy fallow close to his stockaded village. In the afternoon a sheep was slaughtered with due ceremony to ratify the peace with the local elders (*Athuri*), and before sunset our camp was overflowing with cheap provisions of every description—sweet potatoes, yams, green maize, bananas, sugar-cane, honey, eggs, and sleek fowls—the last a luxury we had not tasted since leaving Taveta six weeks before!

---

&lt;!-- Page 288 --&gt;
**288** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On August 28 we made a short march of three hours north-eastward across four parallel ridges and valleys to the district of Waiyaki and Karanja (6,050 feet), camping on a broad turf hill-top overlooking the deep rocky glen of the Ruiruaka brook. Along the whole route the ridges were black with thousands of armed natives, who escorted our column on both flanks with deafening shouts, blowing harsh blasts on curved antelope horns (*corno*) and beating wooden drums, though thanks to the constant exhortations of Miani and Waiyaki, who ran up and down the line waving green branches and haranguing their countrymen at the top of their lungs, not a single hostile act occurred.

No sooner were our tents pitched, however, than elders from half a dozen neighbouring ridges appeared before the camp gate demanding *hongo* and the slaughter of a peace-sheep (*mburi ya thayu*) for each separate hill-top we proposed to cross on the morrow! As this system of endless local blackmail accompanied us right through Kikuyuland from south to north, and was the source of almost all our subsequent troubles, let me explain how the country is governed—or rather lacks all central government. Unlike Useri or Taveta, Kikuyuland possesses neither paramount king nor supreme council of elders; every single narrow mountain ridge (*rugongo*) between two parallel brooks forms an independent miniature republic presided over by its own petty elder or headman (*Muthamaki* or *Muthuri*), whose authority hardly extends five hundred yards beyond his own thorn-hedged village, and is wholly disregarded by the hot-headed young warriors (*Anake*) whenever they have drunk a few calabashes of sour sugar-cane beer (*uhi*)! Consequently, blood-brotherhood or peace ratified with the elders of one ridge had no binding force whatever across the brook half a mile further north: at every brook and every hill-crest a fresh crowd of greedy headmen and drunken, clamorous braves barred the path, demanding coils of iron wire, red cloth, and beads before allowing a single porter to set foot on their fields!

---

&lt;!-- Page 289 --&gt;
*TO KENIA* **289**

Here at Waiyaki's village we had to remain over August 29 and 30, bargaining from morning till night with twelve different elders from the ridges ahead, slaughtering four sheep in successive peace-ceremonies, and buying fresh stores of grain and flour. While waiting here we had ample leisure to observe the dress, ornaments, weapons, and dwellings of the Wakikuyu.

The villages or family homesteads (*itura*) are never built in the open, but are hidden away in dense circular thickets of dark green forest trees and thorny scrub left standing on the backs or shoulders of the ridges. A narrow, crooked tunnel cut through the tangled wall of living bush—so low that one has to stoop nearly double to enter, and closed at night by heavy cross-bars of timber and dry thorn-boughs—leads into a clean, swept courtyard containing from four to ten circular huts with low walls of split planks or wattles plastered with clay and high conical roofs thatched with dry grass and banana leaves, together with several smaller basket-work granaries raised on wooden piles to keep the grain dry and safe from rats. Inside the living-huts a fire smoulders night and day between three stones in the centre of the floor, filling the windowless interior with pungent wood-smoke that escapes slowly through the thatch, while around the wall are partitioned off sleeping-stalls for the women and children and pens for the goats and sheep, which are fed inside the huts on sweet-potato vines and banana stems so carefully that Kikuyu mutton is the fattest and tenderest in all East Africa!

**[Illustration: KIKUYU HOMESTEAD AND GRANARIES]**

Both men and women are passionately fond of ornaments, especially coils of thick brass and iron wire, small iron chains (*munyororo*), and tiny white, red, and blue glass beads. Besides the curious bunches of wooden sticks, brass ear-rings, and bead tassels worn in their distended ear-lobes, the young warriors (*Anake*) smear their bodies and neatly plaited hair with a paste of red ochre and mutton fat or castor oil, paint grotesque white and yellow rings round their eyes and down their shins, and fasten round their ankles heavy iron rattles (*njingiri*) shaped like boat-shells containing small iron balls, which clash rhythmically in their war-dances. Every warrior carries a long leaf-shaped spear with a narrow iron spike at the butt, a short double-edged sword (*ruhiu*) in a red leather scabbard, a wooden club or a knobkerrie formed of a round stone sewn up in shrinking green ox-hide at the end of a tough stick, a painted shield of buffalo hide narrower and more oval than that of the Masai, and a tough wooden bow five feet long with a leather quiver full of reed arrows tipped with barbed iron heads thickly coated with black *Acokanthera* poison and wrapped in soft strips of gazelle skin to keep the poison moist!

---

&lt;!-- Page 290 --&gt;
**290** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On August 31 we broke up our camp at Waiyaki's at 6 A.M., accompanied by our faithful blood-brothers Miani and Waiyaki and eight elders from the northern ridges with whom we had settled *hongo* and eaten peace-mutton the afternoon before. Our route ran north-north-east right across the strike of the mountain spurs descending from the forested ridge of the Aberdare range (*Sattima*) on our left towards the plains of the Athi and Tana rivers on our right. Hardly had we marched twenty minutes along a level ridge when we had to plunge down a precipitous clay path three hundred feet deep into the ravine of the Ruaraka stream, cross the foaming torrent on a slippery log scarcely a foot wide, and haul the loads and horses up an equally steep wall of red clay on the further side! During a four hours' march covering barely five miles in a straight line, we crossed five such deep parallel valleys watered by clear cold mountain streams (Ruaraka, Karura, Gatharaini, Mukuyu, and Kamiti), reaching at 10.30 A.M. the village of the elder Kihara (5,870 feet).

**[Illustration: CROSSING A RAVINE IN KIKUYULAND]**

Throughout the entire march thousands of excited warriors swarmed along the heights on both sides of the path, brandishing spears and bows, blowing war-horns, and screaming threats whenever a weary porter paused for breath or stepped two paces into the edge of a sweet-potato patch to let a companion pass. Twice while our column was stretched out across a steep ravine, bands of drunken young *Anake* rushed down to the stream-bed and attempted to snatch loads from the heads of the hindmost porters, and it was only through the coolness of Count Teleki and Jumbe Kimemeta, who commanded the rear-guard and kept the howling mob at bay with levelled rifles without firing a shot, that a bloody collision was averted!

---

&lt;!-- Page 291 --&gt;
*TO KENIA* **291**

On September 1 we had to halt for a day at Kihara's camp because heavy rain fell throughout the morning, turning the steep red clay paths down the ravines into regular soap-slides on which no laden porter could keep his footing, and also because our earliest guides Miani and Waiyaki declared that they dared not venture any further north away from their own districts, and must first hand us over formally to Kihara and four neighbouring headmen. The whole afternoon was spent in endless speech-making, paying fresh *hongo* of wire, cloth, and beads, and performing the native peace ceremony known as *kuhoria tatha*—in which a ram was suffocated, its stomach opened, and the half-digested green contents mixed with water, honey, and sheep's blood in a wooden trough and sprinkled with a bunch of sacred leaves by the officiating elders over Count Teleki, Kimemeta, and the warrior spokesmen, while everybody chanted a solemn formula invoking destruction upon whoever should draw bow or lift spear against the strangers!

On September 2 we marched four hours north-eastward across four deep ravines (the Ndarugu, Thiririka, Theta, and Ruiru rivers), each running in a rocky bed three hundred to four hundred feet below the crests of the cultivated ridges, and camped at noon on a high open spur in the district of Kahuhia (5,780 feet). Here again the demeanor of the swarming population was extremely turbulent and provocative: although we had paid heavy passage tribute in advance to the elders walking at the head of our column, every fresh hillside was lined with hundreds of painted warriors who yelled insults at our men, spat contemptuously on the ground, and demanded additional beads and wire at the crossings of the streams. While we were pitching our camp at Kahuhia a warrior concealed in a banana grove fifty yards from the fence shot a poisoned arrow at one of our Zanzibari porters who had gone down to the brook for water; fortunately the arrow merely grazed the man's leather cartridge-belt without breaking the skin, and on our threatening to burn the nearest village unless the culprit was punished, the elders brought in two fat sheep as blood-money and drove the turbulent *Anake* back from the camp hill.

---

&lt;!-- Page 292 --&gt;
**292** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On September 3 we crossed the deep gorge of the Komothai and three smaller streams, camping after a toilsome march of three hours and a half near the homestead of an elder named Gathirimu (5,920 feet), where we spent the afternoon as usual negotiating with the headmen of the ridges beyond the Chania river.

On September 4 we descended five hundred feet by a breakneck zigzag track into the picturesque rocky valley of the Chania river (5,350 feet), one of the largest tributaries of the upper Tana, which rushes over a bed of dark basaltic boulders in a foaming torrent five-and-twenty yards wide and three to four feet deep, bridged for foot-passengers by a swaying suspension structure of twisted lianas and split tree-trunks slung between two giant fig-trees! While our porters carried the bales carefully across this shaky aerial bridge, the horses, cattle, and donkeys had to be swum and hauled with ropes through the icy current a hundred yards lower down—a task that occupied fully two hours, under the eyes of two thousand armed natives massed along the steep slopes on both banks. Climbing the northern wall of the Chania valley, we encamped at 11.30 A.M. on a narrow ridge in the district of Getura (5,850 feet), where the inhabitants showed from the very first moment an openly hostile and defiant spirit.

Hardly was our thorn fence finished when several hundred warriors armed with spears, bows, and shields crowded round the camp gate, flatly refusing to sell a single sweet potato or banana, driving back the women who came down with food-baskets, and shouting that we should never pass through their district alive unless we gave every warrior on the ridge a coil of iron wire and a *shuka* of red cloth! To avoid giving the slightest pretext for a quarrel, Count Teleki strictly forbade any of our men to leave the boma, and invited the five chief elders of Getura into the camp to receive a liberal *hongo* and perform the customary peace ceremony over a sheep. The elders pocketed their wire, cloth, and beads greedily, went through the ceremony of sprinkling the sheep's stomach-contents before the gate, and promised to bring guides at dawn on the morrow; yet all night long war-horns sounded from ridge to ridge around us, and our sentries saw dark figures creeping through the banana plantations within fifty paces of our hedge!

---

&lt;!-- Page 293 --&gt;
*TO KENIA* **293**

When morning broke on September 5 not a single promised guide appeared, while the hill-crests to the north and west of our camp were black with at least fifteen hundred to two thousand armed warriors smeared with war-paint and white wood-ash, brandishing their spears and bows and chanting their shrill war-song! At 6.30 A.M., seeing that remaining stationary would only encourage an attack on our cramped position, we formed the caravan in close marching order, with Count Teleki, Jumbe Kimemeta, and fifty picked guns in the van, the cattle and ammunition loads in the centre, and myself with Maktubu, Tom Charles, and forty riflemen covering the rear, and moved slowly up the path leading along the crest of the Getura ridge.

**[Illustration: SKIRMISH ON THE GETURA RIDGE]**

Scarcely had the head of our column advanced four hundred yards between the high thorn hedges and banana groves lining the path when a shower of poisoned arrows whistled out from the thickets on both sides, wounding two of our porters in the shoulder and thigh, while at the same instant several hundred warriors dashed down the slope with poised spears and blood-curdling yells to cut off the rear-guard! Further forbearance would have meant the annihilation of our whole expedition: at a blast of Count Teleki's bugle the porters put down their loads in the middle of the broad pathway, while the vanguard and rear-guard deployed rapidly to right and left and poured three crashing volleys into the charging masses at fifty paces' range!

Stupefied by the deafening roar of the rifles and the whistle of the bullets, and seeing a score of their foremost braves pitch headlong in the grass, the assailants stopped dead, discharged a hasty flight of arrows, and then broke and fled in wild panic down the hillsides and into the fortified bush-villages! While the hospital attendants extracted the arrow-heads from the two wounded porters, tightly ligatured the limbs above the wounds, and cauterized the cuts deeply with nitric acid and ammonia—treatment which happily saved both men's lives—Count Teleki led two storming parties of thirty Somal and Zanzibaris apiece against the three stockaded homesteads from which the treacherous ambuscade had been directed, burst open the barricaded bush-tunnels, drove the defenders headlong down the ravines, and set fire to the huts and granaries as a severe lesson that blood-brotherhood and solemn peace-oaths could not be violated with impunity!

---

&lt;!-- Page 294 --&gt;
**294** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

Pushing on rapidly along the ridge while columns of black smoke rose behind us from the burning villages of Getura, we crossed the steep valley of the Thika river (5,420 feet) without meeting a soul, as the news of the fight and the terrible effect of our firearms had flown like wildfire from hill to hill ahead of us. At 1 P.M. we pitched a strong camp on an open, commanding hilltop at Githambia (5,980 feet), where in the course of the afternoon a deputation of white-haired elders came up unarmed waving huge green banana leaves in token of peace, bringing six fat sheep, three bullocks, and thirty loads of grain and sweet potatoes as a freewill offering, and imploring us not to make war upon their district for the madness of the Getura warriors! Count Teleki received them kindly, paid them full value in beads and wire for their provisions, and assured them that we never fired a shot unless we were first attacked or robbed.

On September 6 and 7 we marched peacefully northward through a succession of magnificent, richly cultivated valleys and ridges watered by the Karinga, Sabasaba, and Maragua rivers, escorted from ridge to ridge by friendly elders carrying green branches who kept the curious crowds of *Anake* at a respectful distance of two hundred yards from our path. The valley of the Maragua river (5,150 feet), which we crossed on the morning of September 7, forms the boundary between southern and central (or northern) Kikuyuland; it is a tremendous rocky gorge over six hundred feet deep, down the bottom of which foams a wild mountain torrent thirty yards wide over ledges of grey gneiss and black basalt, spanned by a rude log bridge supported on piles wedged between the rocks.

Climbing the steep northern wall of the Maragua gorge under a hot sun, we entered the populous district of Kornu (5,840 feet), ruled by a remarkably sensible, dignified old chief named Karanja wa Maragua, who came out a mile along the road to welcome us with fifty elders carrying calabashes of honey-beer and sugar-cane, led us to a capital camping-ground on a breezy turf plateau near his village, and kept such admirable order among his people that during our two days' stay at Kornu (September 7 and 8) not the slightest unpleasantness occurred, while our men bought abundance of flour, beans, yams, bananas, tobacco, and fat sheep at ridiculously low prices!

---

&lt;!-- Page 295 --&gt;
*TO KENIA* **295**

On September 9, escorted by old Karanja and four of his sons, we marched north-north-east for four hours across three deep ravines (the Kahuru, Gikigie, and Mathioya rivers) to the district of Muruka (6,020 feet). While ascending the steep clay path from the foaming Mathioya torrent—which flows at the bottom of a gloomy chasm nearly seven hundred feet below the bounding ridges—a sudden tropical thunderstorm burst over our heads with blinding flashes of lightning and a deluge of icy rain and hail that converted the path within five minutes into a roaring red mud-torrent! Half the porters slipped and rolled down the slope with their loads; the horses and donkeys had to be unloaded and hauled up step by step with ropes; and it was not until three o'clock in the afternoon that our drenched, shivering rear-guard reached the camp on the crest of the Muruka hill!

On September 10 we dried our soaked baggage in the warm morning sun and bought fresh stores of *wimbi* flour and beans from the friendly inhabitants, who brought in several fine bullocks and thirty sheep for sale. From the summit of the Muruka ridge we enjoyed a magnificent view eastward over the lower plains of the upper Tana valley (3,500 to 4,000 feet), across which wound the silver ribbon of the great river bordered by dark green strips of gallery forest, while to the west rose the dark, forest-clad wall of the Aberdare range (*Donyo Sattima*, 13,000 feet), shrouded above nine thousand feet in dense grey cloud-banks.

**[Illustration: VIEW OF THE UPPER TANA VALLEY FROM MURUKA]**

On September 11 we marched three hours and a half across a terribly broken country cut up by four deep mountain torrents (the Gura, Muringato, Chinga, and Gikondi), camping near the village of an elder named Wamugunda (6,150 feet). Here the natives had never even heard of white men, and took our horses for gigantic hornless rhinoceroses or zebras trained to carry loads! Throughout the afternoon thousands of people flocked from all the surrounding hills to gaze at us, sitting in orderly rows fifty yards from our fence and bursting into roars of good-humoured laughter whenever Count Teleki struck a match, puffed cigarette smoke through his nostrils, or fired a revolver shot into a tree-trunk to show them the power of our weapons!

---

&lt;!-- Page 296 --&gt;
**296** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On September 12 and 13 we continued our march northward through the northernmost districts of Kikuyuland—Tetu and Mathira—crossing the deep valleys of the Amboni, Muringato, and Ragati rivers, which flow north-westward and northward before curving round the southern base of Mount Kenia to join the Tana and Sagana! As we approached the northern frontier of Mathira on the afternoon of September 13 (altitude 5,950 feet), the temper of the population again changed for the worse: the warriors of this border region, accustomed to constant bloody feuds with the Masai of Leikipia on the west and the Wambere and Wakamba on the east, were extraordinarily wild, suspicious, and greedy for iron wire and beads. Twice during the march of September 13 bands of four or five hundred painted *Anake* barred the narrow pathway across the ravines with felled trees, demanding immediate payment of twenty coils of iron wire before they would let the column pass, and brandishing their poisoned arrows and spears in our faces until Count Teleki deployed thirty riflemen along the bank and threatened to open fire if the barricade was not cleared away within three minutes!

Camping on the afternoon of September 13 on a bare, round-topped hill in northern Mathira (6,010 feet), we were surrounded all evening by over three thousand turbulent warriors who refused to sell food, tried repeatedly to cut off our water-carriers going down to the Ragati brook at the foot of the slope, and pelted our sentries after dark with stones and clods of earth.

On the morning of September 14, when we endeavoured to come to a peaceful understanding with the local headmen before starting towards the northern boundary forest, the warriors drowned the voices of the elders with frantic war-whoops, shot half a dozen poisoned arrows into the midst of our cattle-kraal—killing one of our pack-donkeys and wounding a Zanzibari porter in the calf—and came charging up the southern and eastern slopes of the hill in three dense columns!

---

&lt;!-- Page 297 --&gt;
*TO KENIA* **297**

Instantly our two hundred riflemen, posted around the circular thorn hedge under the command of Count Teleki, Jumbe Kimemeta, Maktubu, and myself, opened a steady, well-directed fire upon the advancing masses at eighty yards' distance! For two or three minutes the brave savages stood their ground amidst the hail of lead, leaping into the air behind their narrow buffalo-hide shields and launching flights of arrows that fell short against the wind; then, as two War-chiefs wearing tall colobus-monkey head-dresses rolled over dead in the grass and bullets tore through shields and limbs alike, the whole three columns wavered, turned tail, and fled down the hill-slopes across the brooks!

**[Illustration: DEFENCE OF THE CAMP IN MATHIRA]**

Following up the rout immediately with three strong detachments of eighty guns in all, while I remained with the main body to guard the camp and loads, Count Teleki and Jumbe Kimemeta cleared the surrounding ridges within an hour, burned four fortified bush-villages around the base of the hill, and captured sixty head of cattle and two hundred sheep and goats! Before noon a deputation of terrified elders came creeping up the hill holding out green banana-stems and sheep with slit ears (the utmost symbol of submission among the Wakikuyu), begging for mercy and offering to guide us straight out of the cultivated country into the open frontier steppe at the foot of Mount Kenia!

We remained encamped on the hill of Mathira over September 15 and 16 to allow our wounded porter time to recover from the effects of the arrow-poison (which had been promptly counteracted by ligature, excision, and cauterization with ammonia and carbolic acid), and to ratify a durable treaty of peace with twenty elders representing all the northern clans of Mathira and Tetu, who had assembled at the bidding of the local medicine-men.

---

&lt;!-- Page 298 --&gt;
**298** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

This final peace-ratification (*githathi*) on September 16 was performed with extraordinary solemnity in the open meadow below our camp hill, in the presence of over a thousand unarmed natives sitting in a vast semicircle on the grass. A spotless white ram and a black goat were brought into the central ring by four white-haired medicine-men, who first broke all four legs of the animals with heavy wooden clubs and then slit them open longitudinally from throat to tail while each side held an end of the bleeding entrails! Count Teleki, Jumbe Kimemeta, and the six principal Kikuyu chiefs then stepped across the carcasses seven times backwards and forwards, repeating after the head medicine-man an impressive oath that henceforth white men and coast caravans should pass freely and safely through all the lands of Mathira, that food should be sold at fair prices in open market, and that whosoever broke this covenant should be broken and split asunder like the sacrificed sheep and goat! After the ceremony Count Teleki restored to the elders half the captured cattle and sheep, and distributed presents of red cloth, brass wire, and blue beads among the chiefs and medicine-men, transforming our late enemies into such enthusiastic friends that before sunset they brought in a hundred loads of *wimbi* flour, maize, beans, yams, and honey as a parting gift!

On September 17, escorted by twelve Mathira elders and fifty friendly warriors who helped our porters carry the extra grain bags, we marched north-westward for three hours through the park-like belt of northern frontier forest and bush, crossed the clear, rocky stream of the Sagana (upper Tana, 5,680 feet), and emerged with a hearty sigh of relief from the cramped, turbulent valleys of Kikuyuland onto the broad, breezy, uninhabited grassy steppe stretching between the northern end of the Aberdare range (*Sattima*) and the long western foot-slopes of Mount Kenia!

---

&lt;!-- Page 299 --&gt;
*TO KENIA* **299**

What a glorious sense of freedom and security came over us all—leaders and porters alike—as we stepped out once more onto the open, sun-lit turf of the undulating highland plateau (5,800 to 6,200 feet), where the view ranged unhindered for miles over waving grass and park-like clumps of giant junipers and acacias, and where we could march and camp without being hemmed in at every step by howling mobs of drunken, spear-brandishing savages!

**[Illustration: EMERGING FROM KIKUYU FOREST ONTO THE KENIA STEPPE]**

To our left (westward) rose the long, dark, forested wall of the Aberdare range (*Donyo Sattima*), sloping gently down towards the northern plains of Leikipia; right ahead of us to the north-east, its lower forest belt distinctly visible across the rolling steppe though its upper rocky peak was hidden behind a heavy curtain of cumulus clouds, swelled the gigantic dome-like base of Mount Kenia, covering an area fully thirty miles in diameter! Every depression of the grassy plain before us was watered by crystal-clear icy brooks tumbling down from the western snow-fields of Kenia and the eastern flank of Sattima to unite in the Guaso Nyiro (which flows northward and eastward through the plateau of Leikipia) or in the Sagana (which flows southward into the Tana),for our camp on the night of September 17 beside the rocky bed of the Thegu stream (6,050 feet) lay right upon the watershed between the Tana basin and the northern inland drainage of the Guaso Nyiro!

Here on the uninhabited neutral grass-steppe between Kikuyuland and the pastures of the Leikipia Masai, big game abounded once more in incredible numbers: scarcely half a mile from our camp at the Thegu brook Count Teleki shot in the course of an hour's stroll two huge rhinoceroses, a buffalo bull, and three zebras, while along the reedy banks of the stream we bagged a dozen brace of yellow-billed wild ducks and spur-winged geese!

---

&lt;!-- Page 300 --&gt;
**300** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On September 18 our Mathira guides took leave of us with handsome parting presents of wire and beads, pointing out across the gentle grassy slopes to the north-north-east the dark forest edge of the western Kikuyu frontier district of Ndoro, at the immediate foot of Mount Kenia, where they assured us we should find peaceful villages governed by an influential chief named Kihura, with whom coast caravans from Pangani had already traded for ivory and grain.

Marching steadily northward for three hours and a half over rolling grassy downs dotted with isolated groves of lofty *Juniperus procera* and wild olive, and crossing two clear icy streams—the Nairobi and Amboni—which flow westward towards the upper Guaso Nyiro, we pitched our camp on September 18 at an altitude of 6,350 feet beside a beautiful waterfall of the Guaso Niro brook, where the crystal stream leaps forty feet over a cliff of columnar dark grey basalt into a fern-fringed pool surrounded by giant podocarpus and juniper trees.

Towards five o'clock that afternoon, as the sun sank towards the crest of Sattima behind us, a cool easterly breeze suddenly swept away the dense bank of clouds that had veiled the upper slopes of Kenia all day, and for the first time Mount Kenia stood revealed before our eyes in all its solitary, awe-inspiring majesty! Above the broad, gently rising pedestal of dark green primeval forest and bamboo jungle (which clothes the mountain all round up to an altitude of ten thousand five hundred feet), and above a yellowish-green zone of steep grassy alp and moss-grown crags, towered at a distance of fifteen miles to the east-north-east a central rugged pyramid of dark brown and black rock, rent into two sharp culminating peaks sheeted in dazzling snow and gleaming blue-green glaciers, which flushed delicate rose-pink and gold in the rays of the setting sun!

**[Illustration: FIRST VIEW OF MOUNT KENIA FROM THE GUASO NIRO CAMP]**

Unlike Kibo on Kilimanjaro, which presents the regular, dome-like outline of a snow-capped volcanic crater where the ice-mantle rests unbroken upon the broad summit plateau, the central pinnacle of Kenia is a ruined volcanic plug or core of ancient crystalline rock (*phonolite* and *kenyte*), whose outer crater-wall has been completely worn away by centuries of erosion, leaving the jagged central Tooth towering nearly three thousand feet above the surrounding snow-slopes to an altitude of over eighteen thousand feet above the sea! Hence the appropriateness of the Masai name for the mountain—*Donyo Egere*, 'the Spotted or Dappled Mountain'—because on the precipitous upper cliffs of the central pyramid the snow can only lodge in wide couloirs, ledges, and corries between black, vertical walls of naked rock!

---

&lt;!-- Page 301 --&gt;
*TO KENIA* **301**

On September 19 a short march of two hours and a half across rich mountain pastures watered by three rushing brooks brought us to the southern border of the cleared and cultivated district of Ndoro (6,380 feet), situated in a deep, bay-like recess cut out of the dense primeval forest at the western base of Mount Kenia. Pitching our tents on a wide, level grassy terrace beside the rushing, icy torrent of the Guaso Narok, about half a mile outside the thorny stockades and banana groves of the nearest native villages, we immediately set the whole caravan to work felling timber and building a permanent strongly fortified camp, as we knew we should have to remain here for several weeks to rest the men, purchase large supplies of food for our further journey northward across the uninhabited plains of Leikipia to Lake Baringo, and carry out our projected scientific exploration and ascent of Mount Kenia!

Let me now go back and trace more fully from my daily journal the varied experiences of our three weeks' march through Kikuyuland (from August 27 to September 18), together with the geography and ethnography of this remarkable mountain land, which we were the first Europeans ever to traverse from south to north.

As already indicated, the highland plateau of Kikuyu forms a narrow, elongated volcanic terrace stretching for about ninety miles along the eastern flank of the Aberdare range (*Sattima*) from Donyo Lamuyu (1° 25' S. lat.) to the western foot of Mount Kenia (0° 15' S. lat.), with an average elevation along our line of march of from 5,500 to 6,400 feet above the sea. Geologically the whole region consists of thick sheets of basaltic lava, phonetic trachytes, and decomposed volcanic tuffs piled up against the ancient gneiss foundation of East Africa during the stupendous volcanic eruptions that accompanied the subsidence of the neighbouring Rift Valley, and subsequently carved by the parallel streams pouring down from the wet crest of Sattima into scores of narrow, steep-sided ridges (*migongo*) running with wonderful regularity from west-north-west to east-south-east!

---

&lt;!-- Page 302 --&gt;
**302** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

To give some idea of the extraordinary number of perennial water-courses that furrow this mountain terrace, I need only mention that during our seventeen actual marching days between Ngongo Bagas and Ndoro—covering a direct distance of barely seventy-five geographical miles—our caravan had to descend into and climb out of no fewer than sixty-eight deep valleys and ravines, each traversed at the bottom by a crystal-clear, rushing brook or river from ten to a hundred feet wide! All these streams—of which the principal from south to north are the Ruaraka, Karura, Ruiru, Ndarugu, Komothai, Chania, Thika, Maragua, Mathioya, Gura, Chinga, Amboni, and Sagana—belong (with the exception of the last three brooks north of the Thegu watershed) to the drainage system of the upper Tana and Athi rivers, uniting in the lower plains to the east of Kikuyuland to form the noble stream of the Tana (or Kiloluma), which sweeps in a wide curve round the southern and eastern base of Mount Kenia on its way to the Indian Ocean.

Thanks to this abundant irrigation, to the rich chocolate-red volcanic soil formed by the weathering of the lavas and tuffs, and to a cool, healthy temperate climate where the thermometer rarely rises above 78° Fahr. at midday or sinks below 45° Fahr. at night, Kikuyuland is one of the most fertile and densely peopled agricultural regions in all Equatorial Africa! Originally the entire country from the foot of Donyo Lamuyu to the slopes of Sattima and Kenia was covered with continuous primeval forest (*Subugo*), such as still forms the protective frontier belt around the borders and clothes the upper slopes of Sattima above seven thousand five hundred feet; in the course of generations, however, the industrious Wakikuyu have cleared away the giant timber over the whole central zone between five thousand and seven thousand feet—leaving only dense circular rings of forest around their hidden villages (*itura*) and occasional sacred fig-trees (*mugumo*) on the crests of the hills—and have transformed the steep ridges into an unbroken expanse of neat fields and plantations!

---

&lt;!-- Page 303 --&gt;
*TO KENIA* **303**

Let me review day by day from my diary the details of our progress through this labyrinth of cultivated hills and valleys after leaving Ngongo Bagas on August 27.

When our caravan filed into the dark frontier forest path on the morning of August 27, led by our three blood-brothers Miani, Karanja, and Waiyaki, we found the narrow track obstructed every two or three hundred yards by low breastworks of felled tree-trunks and thorny brushwood (*kihingo*), guarded on the inner side by small detachments of fifteen or twenty Wakikuyu warriors armed with bows, poisoned arrows, and long spears, who watched our long column scramble over the barriers with mingled curiosity and suspicion. In the middle of the forest belt, which is here about three miles wide, we crossed the head-waters of the Nairobi and Mathari brooks, flowing in deep, fern-hung channels beneath towering podocarpus, juniper (*Juniperus procera*), and olive trees whose trunks measured five to seven feet in diameter.

Emerging from the northern edge of the timber at 8 A.M. into the bright sunshine of the open cultivated ridges (6,300 feet), we marched for two hours and a half through thriving fields of sweet potatoes, maize, beans, and sugar-cane to the village of Miani (6,250 feet), where we pitched our first camp in Kikuyuland on a grassy common surrounded by thickets of wild roses, brambles, and yellow-flowered cassias. Scarcely were our loads stacked in two long walls when old Miani appeared with three fellow-elders leading a fat white ram for the ceremony of *kuhoria tatha* (sprinkling the stomach-contents to ensure peace across the district). While the old men chanted their responsive litany over the slaughtered animal—one elder reciting sentence after sentence in a loud voice and the whole assembled crowd of three hundred natives answering in chorus '*Thayu! Thayu!*' ('Peace! Peace!')—our men opened market outside the thorn hedge and bought within two hours over a hundred loads of sweet potatoes, yams, bananas, maize-flour, and sugar-cane, besides forty live fowls and several pots of clear yellowish honey!

**[Illustration: KIKUYU IRON SWORDS, SPEARS, AND ARROWS]**

---

&lt;!-- Page 304 --&gt;
**304** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On August 28 we marched north-eastward from Miani's across four steep ridges and valleys (the Mathari, Gitathuru, Kiu, and Ruiruaka brooks) to the district of Waiyaki and Karanja (6,050 feet), where we remained encamped over August 29 and 30. During these two days I had an opportunity of inspecting Waiyaki's own village, situated inside a dense circular grove of tall forest trees about four hundred yards west of our camp. Passing through a narrow, winding pathway cut through fifty yards of impenetrable thorny underwood and barred midway by a strong gate of heavy split planks resting in grooved posts, I entered a neat circular clearing containing eight huts belonging to Waiyaki, his six wives, and his married son, together with ten smaller bee-hive-shaped granaries perched on stone and wooden stilts.

Behind the huts, inside the shelter of the forest ring, several smiths (*Aturi*) were hard at work forging spear-heads, double-edged swords (*ruhiu*), axes, and agricultural knives out of iron ore smelted in shallow clay furnaces from the rich ferruginous sand washed down by the mountain streams. Their bellows consisted of two cones of dressed goat-skin sewn together into a wide wooden nozzle fitted into a clay tuyere, worked alternately up and down by a boy squatting behind the charcoal fire, while the master smith held the glowing iron with rude tongs upon a flat granite boulder serving as anvil, and beat it into shape with a solid oval hammer of iron! Considering the primitiveness of their tools, the finish and symmetry of the Kikuyu weapons—especially the long, leaf-shaped spears with slender central ribs and the two-edged swords with red leather sheaths and bead-worked belts—are truly astonishing.

Besides iron-working, the Wakikuyu excel in dressing skins as soft as chamois leather, weaving tough network bags (*kiondo*) in tasteful striped patterns from the bark-fibre of several forest shrubs, making coarse earthenware cooking-pots, and hollowing out wooden bee-hives (*mwatū*) four feet long from sections of tree-trunks, which they brand with their family clan-marks and hang up by dozens in the branches of the high trees around the villages and in the frontier forest. From the abundant honey harvested twice a year from these tree-hives, as well as from the juice of the sugar-cane crushed in wooden mortars, they brew enormous quantities of sour, heady beer (*njohi* or *uhi*), storing it in huge calabashes two feet in diameter near the hut fires until it ferments briskly. Unfortunately for the peace of the country and the safety of passing caravans, whenever the grain harvest is gathered in August and September, elders and warriors alike indulge in continuous drinking-bouts lasting for days together, during which they lose all self-control and pick bloody quarrels on the slightest provocation!

---

&lt;!-- Page 305 --&gt;
*TO KENIA* **305**

Let me describe the agriculture and field implements of the Wakikuyu as we saw them on every hillside around Waiyaki's and throughout our march northward. Unlike most Bantu tribes, who use broad iron hoes for tilling the ground, the Wakikuyu break up new forest land and cultivate their fields almost entirely with long, pointed digging-sticks of hard heavy wood (*muro*) seven to eight feet long and two inches thick, wielded by three or four men standing abreast who drive the sharp points deep into the turf together and lever up huge clods two feet square, while the women follow behind with broad-bladed iron knives (*kahiu*) and short wooden clubs, breaking the clods fine, picking out the weeds and grass-roots, and planting the seed in neat parallel rows!

**[Illustration: KIKUYU AGRICULTURAL IMPLEMENTS AND GRANARY]**

Every cultivated ridge displays a regular zoning of crops from top to bottom according to soil and moisture: on the broad, breezy crests and upper slopes stand waving fields of millet (*mtama* and small red *wimbi*), maize, and sweet-scented peas and beans (*njahi* and *mboso*); along the middle slopes stretch wide plots of sweet potatoes (*ngwaci*) and yams trained up tall wooden stakes like hops, interspersed with flourishing plantations of green banana trees (*marigu*) and patches of tobacco; while in the rich, moist black soil at the bottom of the valleys alongside the rushing brooks grow dense beds of large-leaved taro (*nduma*, *Colocasia antiquorum*) and tall thickets of purple and yellow sugar-cane ten to twelve feet high! Around every homestead sheep and goats are kept in small numbers, feeding inside the huts on sweet-potato tops and banana peelings, while cattle are comparatively scarce in southern and central Kikuyuland on account of the lack of open grazing-grounds and the constant fear of raids from the Masai.

---

&lt;!-- Page 306 --&gt;
**306** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On August 31, after two days of interminable palavers and *hongo* payments at Waiyaki's, we resumed our march northward across the steep ravines of the Ruaraka, Karura, Gatharaini, Mukuyu, and Kamiti streams to Kihara's village (5,870 feet), where we were detained over September 1 by heavy morning rain and the return home of our southern guides Miani and Waiyaki. Here at Kihara's Count Teleki gave an impressive exhibition of firearms before two thousand assembled warriors and elders who had begun to hustle our water-carriers at the brook: having set up an old wooden shield covered with sheet-iron from an empty biscuit-tin against a banana stem at a hundred paces' distance, he sent three solid Express bullets crashing clean through shield, iron plate, and three banana trunks behind it, and then sent up two war-rockets after dark that hissed in fiery arcs across the valley and burst with thunderous reports over the opposite hillside, sending the whole multitude flying home to their stockades in speechless awe!

On September 2 we crossed the deep valleys of the Ndarugu, Thiririka, Theta, and Ruiru rivers to Kahuhia (5,780 feet), and on September 3 the gorge of the Komothai and three smaller brooks to Gathirimu's (5,920 feet). Throughout both days' marches the steep ridges were lined with thousands of excited, half-tipsy warriors smeared with red clay, white chalk, and charcoal, who ran alongside our column blowing antelope horns, rattling their iron ankle-bells, and demanding beads and wire at every narrow log-bridge across the torrents.

On September 4 we crossed the deep basaltic gorge of the Chania river (5,350 feet) by the swaying liana and log suspension bridge already described, and climbed up the steep northern slope to the turbulent district of Getura (5,850 feet), where on the morning of September 5 the treacherous ambuscade and arrow attack upon our column forced us in self-defence to storm and burn three stockaded villages and drive our assailants headlong over the ridges!

---

&lt;!-- Page 307 --&gt;
*TO KENIA* **307**

Regarding the poisoned arrows used against us by the Wakikuyu in this fight at Getura and subsequently at Mathira on September 14, we ascertained that the black, pitch-like poison (*muriju*) smeared thickly behind the barbed iron head or wooden point is prepared by the Wakamba and Wakikuyu medicine-men by boiling down the chipped wood, roots, and bark of the *Acokanthera Schimperi* tree (mixed sometimes with euphoria juice and dried snakes' heads) for twenty-four hours in an earthen pot until it forms a black, viscid extract with a sweetish, licorice-like smell. When fresh and moist, this poison acts swiftly as a powerful cardiac paralyzant, killing a man or a large antelope within fifteen to thirty minutes unless the arrow-head is instantly cut out, the limb tightly corded above the wound, and the puncture thoroughly washed out with ammonia, carbolic acid, or caustic! Fortunately for our three wounded porters (two at Getura and one at Mathira), our hospital chest was always carried at the head of the column, so that within two minutes of being struck their wounds were ligatured, incised, and cauterized with strong ammonia and nitric acid, and all three men recovered completely within ten days!

After the sharp lesson administered at Getura, our marches on September 6, 7, and 8 across the Thika, Karinga, Sabasaba, and Maragua rivers into the central district of Kornu (5,840 feet) passed off in perfect harmony, thanks above all to the friendly, intelligent chief Karanja wa Maragua, who kept his people in exemplary order and accompanied us himself on September 9 across the deep chasms of the Kahuru, Gikigie, and Mathioya torrents to Muruka (6,020 feet).

On September 11 we crossed four more steep mountain streams—the Gura, Muringato, Chinga, and Gikondi—into the northern district of Tetu (6,150 feet), and on September 12 and 13 traversed the valleys of the Amboni and Ragati into northern Mathira (6,010 feet), where on September 14 we had to repel the fierce attack of three thousand warriors, storm four villages, and finally conclude on September 16 the solemn treaty of peace (*githathi*) over the broken and bisected sheep and goat before emerging on September 17 across the Sagana river onto the open western steppe at the foot of Mount Kenia!

---

&lt;!-- Page 308 --&gt;
**308** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

Let me now resume our diary from the morning of September 19, when we pitched our permanent camp on the grassy terrace beside the icy Guaso Narok torrent in the western Kikuyu border settlement of Ndoro (6,380 feet), right at the western base of Mount Kenia.

Here at Ndoro the primeval forest (*Subugo*) which clothes the entire lower slopes of Kenia recedes eastward for two or three miles, leaving a fertile, sheltered bay of rolling hills and valleys watered by the Guaso Narok, Guaso Nairobi, and several smaller mountain brooks pouring down from the snow-fields above.
