This favoured recess is inhabited by a prosperous colony of northern Wakikuyu under the mild rule of an old chief or spokesman named Kihura, who had twice before traded peacefully with large Swahili ivory caravans from Pangani under Jumbe Kimemeta, and who came down to our camp gate within an hour of our arrival accompanied by twenty elders bringing presents of three fat sheep, four jars of honey, and thirty baskets of *wimbi* flour, maize, beans, and yams!

After performing the customary ceremony of *kuhoria tatha* over a white sheep and receiving a handsome present of red and white cloth, iron and brass wire, and blue beads, old Kihura proclaimed a public daily market on the green meadow fifty paces outside our thorn stockade, where from September 20 onwards two or three hundred women and girls assembled quietly every morning between eight and twelve o'clock to barter grain, flour, beans, sweet potatoes, yams, bananas, sugar-cane, tobacco, and honey for small beads and wire!

---

&lt;!-- Page 309 --&gt;
*TO KENIA* **309**

Even after our arrival at Ndoro, however, the suspicious, unruly nature of the younger Kikuyu warriors (*Anake*) showed itself repeatedly during the first fortnight of our stay, requiring constant vigilance and firmness on our part to prevent an open rupture before our large stock of sixty days' grain rations for the onward journey to Lake Baringo and Samburuland had been safely collected and stored in our camp sheds.

Thus on September 22 and 23 several hundred warriors from the neighbouring ridges of northern Mathira and eastern Ndoro came swaggering down to the edge of the market meadow in full war-paint, driving away the women who were selling flour to our men and demanding that we should pay a fresh *hongo* of iron wire to every warrior clan in the district! Count Teleki immediately had the camp gate closed, drew up fifty Somal and Askari with fixed bayonets along the stockade, and sent word to Kihura that if the turbulent youths did not clear off within ten minutes and leave the market women in peace, he would treat Ndoro exactly as he had treated Getura and Mathira! This firm language, backed up by the reports which our Mathira guides had given of the fight on September 14, worked wonders: old Kihura and his fellow-elders rushed out with long sticks, belaboured the noisy *Anake* over the shoulders, and drove them back across the Guaso Narok, after which the women returned smiling to their baskets and trading proceeded without a hitch!

From September 24 to October 7 the work of buying, drying, pounding, and packing maize, *wimbi* flour, and beans went on briskly every day inside our fortified camp, while our smiths and carpenters repaired the iron boat-sections, pack-saddles, and ammunition boxes, and our convalescent patients grew plump and strong on the rich mountain fare and bracing air of the Kenia highlands. During this fortnight the weather at Ndoro was cool, cloudy, and unsettled: every morning broke clear and fresh with a minimum temperature of 44° to 48° Fahr., showing the glittering snow-peaks and glaciers of Kenia towering sharp and cloudless against the deep blue eastern sky until about eight o'clock, when fleecy mists began to form around the bamboo belt at ten thousand feet, rapidly thickening into dense dark grey clouds that veiled the whole upper mountain for the rest of the day and discharged heavy afternoon showers of cold rain over the forest and foot-hills!

By the evening of October 7 we had accumulated and packed in strong canvas and leather bags over a hundred and eighty loads of dry grain, beans, and flour—sufficient for two months' full rations for our whole caravan—besides forty head of cattle and two hundred sheep and goats, so that everything was ready for Count Teleki's long-planned ascent of Mount Kenia before setting out across the steppes of Leikipia to Lake Baringo!


---

&lt;!-- Page 362 --&gt;

### CHAPTER VI

#### STAY AT NDORO. ASCENT OF MOUNT KENIA. JOURNEY THROUGH LEIKIPIA AND TRIP TO LAKE BARINGO

*(From October 8 to December 7, 1887)*

> Stay at Ndoro—Ascent of Mount Kenia—Character of the vegetation on the slopes of Kenia—Return to Ndoro—Departure from Ndoro—On the Guaso Nyiro—Across the high plateau of Leikipia—The Ongalea mountains—Parting of the caravan—Wait in the Larabwal Valley—Arrival at Njemps near Lake Baringo.

**[Illustration: BAMBOO LANCE AND QUIVER FROM MOUNT KENIA]**

ON the morning of October 8 everything was ready for our excursion up the unknown slopes of Mount Kenia, which no white man's foot had ever yet trodden, as the late Dr. Krapf had only seen the snow-clad summit from a great distance in Ukambani in December 1849, while Joseph Thomson in October 1883 had merely passed rapidly along its western base across the plateau of Leikipia without attempting an ascent. As I was unfortunately suffering at the time from a sharp attack of dysentery and fever brought on by the cold, wet weather at Ndoro, it was decided that I should remain behind in charge of the main camp and baggage, while Count Teleki undertook the ascent accompanied by Jumbe Kimemeta, Qualla, thirty-five picked Zanzibari porters carrying warm blankets, reserve clothing, Alpine tent, boiling-point thermometers, aneroids, photographic camera, and twelve days' rations of roast maize-meal (*posho*), together with four Wakikuyu guides furnished by old Chief Kihura to lead the party through the lower belt of primeval forest.

---

&lt;!-- Page 363 --&gt;
*ASCENT OF MOUNT KENIA AND JOURNEY TO BARINGO* **363**

Setting out from Ndoro camp (6,380 feet) at 6.30 A.M. on October 8, Count Teleki and his party marched eastward for an hour across cleared fields and bracken-covered hillocks, and at 7.30 A.M. plunged into the dark, dank wall of virgin forest (*Subugo*) clothing the lower western flank of the mountain. Here they followed a narrow, scarcely perceptible game- and honey-hunters' track winding steadily upwards along the crest of a steep spur between two rushing mountain torrents. Giant podocarpus, junipers (*Juniperus procera*), wild olives, and Buttneriaceae rising to a height of a hundred and twenty to a hundred and fifty feet formed a dense canopy overhead through which hardly a ray of sunlight penetrated, while their massive trunks were draped in dripping cushions of green moss and liverworts, and the ground beneath was choked with rotting timber, giant stinging-nettles, tree-ferns, and thorny brambles.

At 1 P.M., at an altitude of 8,100 feet, the lower timber forest began to pass rapidly into a belt of gigantic bamboos (*Arundinaria alpina*), whose hollow green stems, two to four inches thick and thirty to fifty feet high, grew so closely packed together that the Wakikuyu guides had to hew a tunnel step by step with their broad iron knives (*sime*), while cold water dripped incessantly from the feathery foliage upon the shivering porters! At 3.30 P.M., soaked to the skin by a heavy afternoon downpour, the party pitched camp at an altitude of 8,850 feet in a small damp glade beside a cold brook in the middle of the bamboo jungle, where the four Wakikuyu guides refused to go a step further on the morrow, declaring that the upper regions of the mountain above the bamboos were the abode of the great mountain spirit *Ngai*, where any mortal who ventured would be frozen into stone!

---

&lt;!-- Page 364 --&gt;
**364** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On October 9 Count Teleki pushed on without guides straight up the steep western ridge through the dripping bamboo forest, which grew denser and wetter up to nine thousand five hundred feet and then gradually thinned out and became mixed with gnarled, moss-bearded podocarpus and Hagenia trees (*Brayera anthelmintica*) festooned with long grey streamers of *Usnea* lichen. At noon, at an altitude of 10,500 feet, the last clumps of bamboo and timber were left behind, and the party stepped out onto the lower edge of the open Alpine grass and moorland zone! Here the mountain slope rose at a gentle inclination of 12° to 15°, covered with thick tussocks of wiry mountain grass, boggy patches of sphagnum moss, tree-heaths (*Erica arborea*), silver-leaved immortelles (*Helichrysum*), alpine lady's-mantle (*Alchemilla*), and the grotesque, palm-like trunks of the giant groundsels (*Senecio Johnstoni*) and pillar-like lobelias (*Lobelia Telekii* and *Lobelia Deckeni*), whose tall flower-spikes rose eight to twelve feet above the boggy turf!

**[Illustration: VEGETATION IN THE ALPINE ZONE OF MOUNT KENIA (LOBELIA TELEKII AND SENECIO JOHNSTONI)]**

After five hours' toilsome climbing ankle-deep through the icy mud and spongy moss-bogs under a cold drizzle of rain and sleet, Count Teleki pitched his second mountain camp at 2.30 P.M. at an altitude of 11,600 feet under the shelter of an overhanging cliff of grey trachytic rock beside a head-stream of the Guaso Narok. During the night the sky cleared completely and the thermometer sank to 25° Fahr. (-4° C.), freezing the wet tents as stiff as boards and covering every pool and bog with a crust of ice half an inch thick, to the speechless amazement of the Zanzibari porters, who carried slabs of the 'white stone' to their camp-fires to warm them and saw them vanish into water in their hands!

---

&lt;!-- Page 365 --&gt;
*ASCENT OF MOUNT KENIA AND JOURNEY TO BARINGO* **365**

On October 10, leaving twenty porters behind in the camp at 11,600 feet to guard the heavy loads and keep fires burning of dead *Senecio* trunks, Count Teleki ascended with Qualla, Jumbe Kimemeta, and fifteen of the hardiest men up the steepening valley of the Guaso Narok towards the base of the central rocky peak, which now towered clear and sharp against the morning sky ahead. Up to twelve thousand five hundred feet the boggy slopes were still dotted with giant *Senecios* fifteen to twenty feet high and spiked *Lobelia Telekii*; above that elevation vegetation became restricted to low tufts of brownish-yellow grass, grey lichens, silvery *Helichrysum* bushes, and cushions of moss nestled in the clefts of the glaciated bosses of grey phonolite and kenyte rock.

At 1 P.M. Count Teleki established his highest bivouac at an altitude of 13,100 feet under a huge erratic boulder near the head of the western valley, where the thermometer stood at 36° Fahr. in the afternoon shade and fell during the night to 18.5° Fahr. (-7.5° C.)!

At daybreak on October 11, accompanied only by Qualla and six Zanzibaris wrapped in thick woollen jerseys, blankets, and mountain boots, the Count set out for the final assault upon the central peak. Climbing steadily up steep slopes of frozen volcanic débris, ice-worn rock-ledges, and hard *névé* snow, the Zanzibaris one after another sank down exhausted by mountain-sickness and the biting frost at thirteen thousand eight hundred feet; even faithful Qualla had to halt on a sunny rock-ledge at fourteen thousand two hundred feet, while Count Teleki pushed on entirely alone up the steep western ridge of the outer crater-ring (*crater-wall*).

**[Illustration: CENTRAL PEAK AND GLACIERS OF MOUNT KENIA FROM 15,000 FEET]**

At eleven o'clock in the morning of October 11, 1887, after scaling a precipitous wall of dark phonolitic rock sheeted in snow and ice-glaze, Count Teleki stood upon the jagged crest of the western crater-rim at an altitude of 15,355 feet (4,680 metres) above the sea—the snow-line on the western face of Kenia lying at 14,750 feet!

---

&lt;!-- Page 366 --&gt;
**366** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

Directly at his feet the cliff plunged down five hundred feet sheer into a circular caldron or ancient crater-basin (*kar*) nearly a mile wide, completely filled with a magnificent, deeply crevassed sea of blue-green glacier ice, fed by steep hanging glaciers and snow-couloirs descending from the vertical black precipices of the twin central pinnacles, which soared in inaccessible walls of naked rock nearly three thousand feet higher to an estimated total altitude of 18,400 to 19,000 feet! Before the clouds boiled up from the valleys at noon and blotted out the view, the Count secured two capital photographs of the central peak and glaciers, boiled his hypsometer thermometers, collected geological specimens of the glassy dark-grey *kenyte* lava and phonolite forming the crater-wall, and then rejoined Qualla and the porters at the bivouac at 13,100 feet by 3 P.M.!

Descending rapidly through the Alpine moorland and bamboo zones on October 12 and 13—shooting on the way at ten thousand feet in the upper bamboo forest a fine male black-and-white Colobus monkey (*Colobus guereza caudatus*) and observing fresh tracks of elephants and buffaloes as high as eleven thousand five hundred feet!—Count Teleki and his party marched back into our main camp at Ndoro on the afternoon of October 14 in splendid health and spirits!

During the Count's absence on the mountain I had completely recovered from my indisposition, had taken a series of astronomical observations for latitude and magnetic variation at Ndoro, and had finished buying and packing the last loads of grain and beans. Still, we had to remain at Ndoro for five days more (October 15 to 19) to hire thirty extra Wakikuyu carriers from Chief Kihura to help transport our heavy flour loads across the first five marches of the uninhabited Leikipia plateau, and to wait until the northern frontier elders had completed their farewell peace-ceremonies.

---

&lt;!-- Page 367 --&gt;
*ASCENT OF MOUNT KENIA AND JOURNEY TO BARINGO* **367**

On October 20, 1887, at sunrise we finally bade farewell to our friendly hosts at Ndoro and set out north-westward across the open grassy plateau towards the upper course of the Guaso Nyiro and the tableland of Leikipia. Our caravan was now laden like a harvest-wain: in addition to his regular sixty-pound bale or box, every one of our two hundred and seventy porters carried twenty days' rations of *wimbi* flour and beans strapped on top of his load, while thirty hired Wakikuyu from Ndoro carried sixty extra bags of grain and drove before them our herd of forty-five sleek bullocks and two hundred and twenty fat sheep and goats!

For the first four days (October 20 to 23) our route led north-westward and northward across a magnificent, gently undulating park-land (6,000 to 6,400 feet) connecting the northern foot of the Aberdare range (*Sattima*) with the western spurs of Mount Kenia. Every morning we crossed one or more crystal-clear mountain torrents rushing down over beds of dark basalt and gneiss from the western glaciers of Kenia to join the main stream of the Guaso Nyiro—notably the Guaso Narok, Guaso Rongai, Burguret, Nanyuki, Likii, and Sirimon—their banks lined with noble gallery forests of giant junipers (*Juniperus procera*), podocarpus, wild olives, and acacias, while the intervening grassy downs swarmed with herds of buffaloes, rhinoceroses, zebras, elands, hartebeests (*Alcelaphus Jacksoni*), waterbucks, and Thomson's gazelles!

**[Illustration: CAMP ON THE UPPER GUASO NYIRO]**

On October 24 we turned west-north-west and descended gradually through thinner mimosa bush and dry yellow grass to the main stream of the upper Guaso Nyiro (5,650 feet), which collects all the waters from the northern slopes of Sattima and the western face of Kenia, and flows here in a rocky bed thirty yards wide and two to three feet deep between shady banks of tall acacias, wild date-palms, and green bush. Here our thirty hired Wakikuyu carriers put down their grain-bags, received their stipulated pay in iron wire and beads, and returned home to Ndoro, as they dared not venture across the Guaso Nyiro into the dreaded grazing-grounds of the Leikipia Masai.

---

&lt;!-- Page 368 --&gt;
**368** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

From October 25 to November 2 we marched steadily north-westward across the rolling, breezy highland plateau of Leikipia at an elevation of between 6,000 and 6,800 feet above the sea. Twenty years before our visit the rich pastures of Leikipia were inhabited by the most powerful and numerous section of the northern Wakwafi or Masai (*Il Leikipiak*), whose herds of cattle and sheep numbered hundreds of thousands; after a long series of sanguinary civil wars, however, the combined southern Masai clans of Naivasha, Kisongo, and Kapotei utterly routed the Leikipiak, captured their herds, and drove the remnant of the tribe either to take refuge among the agricultural settlements at Njemps and Taveta or to wander as poor hunting outcasts (*Wandorobbo*) in the forests of Kenia and Mau!

Consequently throughout our nine days' march across this glorious highland tableland we did not meet a single human being except two small wandering families of half-starved *Wandorobbo* elephant-hunters, though on every hillside we came upon the circular rings of green turf marking the sites of old Masai cattle-kraals and bleached heaps of ox- and buffalo-skulls resulting from the great cattle-plague and tribal wars! In the absence of man and domestic herds, wild game had taken complete possession of the plateau in almost incredible numbers: scarcely an hour passed on the march without our sighting three or four rhinoceroses standing asleep under solitary mimosa trees or trotting upwind with comical pig-like snorts straight towards our column; vast herds of buffaloes—often three to five hundred strong—lay chewing the cud in the marshy reed-swamps (such as the great swamp of Marmanet or Pesi, 6,350 feet, which we skirted on October 29 and 30); while elephants, giraffes, elands, zebras, Jackson's hartebeests, waterbucks, and gazelles grazed in every grassy valley!

**[Illustration: BUFFALO HERD IN THE PESI SWAMP, LEIKIPIA]**

To relieve our heavily weighted porters of part of their double loads of grain and to feed the whole camp continuously on fresh meat, Count Teleki and I hunted every morning and afternoon along the line of march, bagging between October 20 and November 4 across the Kenia and Leikipia steppes no fewer than nineteen rhinoceroses, fourteen buffaloes, one huge bull elephant (shot by the Count on November 1 near the Ongalea hills with tusks weighing a hundred and seventy pounds!), three giraffes, twenty-two zebras, eight elands, and thirty-five antelopes and gazelles!

---

&lt;!-- Page 369 --&gt;
*ASCENT OF MOUNT KENIA AND JOURNEY TO BARINGO* **369**

On November 3 we reached the northern rim of the western Leikipia plateau near the picturesque rugged gneiss hills of Ongalea (6,500 feet), whence we looked down to the west into the tremendous abyss of the Great Rift Valley, three thousand feet below us, with the silvery sheet of Lake Baringo shining in the bottom of the trough thirty miles away to the west-south-west, backed by the towering blue wall of the Kamasia and Elgeyo escarpments!

As we still had over sixty extra loads of grain and beads which could only be carried forward by marching in double relays, and as our path down the precipitous, rocky lava terraces of the Leikipia escarpment into the Rift Valley was frightfully rough and waterless, Count Teleki decided on November 4 to divide our caravan temporarily at the pleasant wooded valley of Larabwal (5,820 feet) on the upper bench of the escarpment: leaving me behind at Larabwal in a strongly stockaded camp with one hundred and seventy men, the sick and footsore porters, the cattle, and the whole of the surplus baggage and grain reserves, the Count marched down to Njemps near Lake Baringo on November 5 with Jumbe Kimemeta and one hundred picked porters, intending to establish our base camp at Njemps, send back empty carriers a week later to fetch me and the remaining loads down from Larabwal, and investigate the routes leading northward from Baringo to the unknown lakes of Samburuland!

During the twelve days that I remained encamped with my detachment in the lonely highland valley of Larabwal (November 5 to 16), our camp was transformed into a regular hunters' paradise and butcher's drying-yard: game of every kind—buffaloes, rhinoceroses, elands, waterbucks, zebras, and bushbucks—came morning and evening to drink at the chain of clear pools in the rocky brook below our thorn boma, while lions and leopards roared around our cattle-kraal every night! In the course of twelve days' shooting within four miles of Larabwal camp I killed with my own rifle eleven buffaloes, four rhinoceroses, six elands, nine waterbucks, twelve zebras, and fourteen smaller antelopes, drying thousands of pounds of choice buffalo- and eland-beef (*charqui*) on wooden frames over smoky fires and packing it in bundles to take down to the barren trough of Baringo!

---

&lt;!-- Page 370 --&gt;
**370** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On November 15 Qualla and Maktubu arrived at Larabwal from Njemps with ninety rested porters sent up by Count Teleki to carry down our extra loads, and at daybreak on November 17 we broke up our pleasant hunting camp and began the steep, rugged descent of nearly three thousand feet down the broken volcanic steps of the Leikipia escarpment into the Baringo basin. For three days (November 17, 18, and 19) we scrambled down frightfully rough cattle- and game-tracks over sheer ledges of dark basaltic and trachytic lava strewn with loose, sharp-edged boulders and overgrown with dry, leafless thorn-scrub (*Acacia mellifera* and *wait-a-bit* thorns) that tore clothes and skin to ribbons, camping by scanty pools of warm, greenish water in the rocky ravines of Maza (4,650 feet) and Mukutan (3,850 feet).

Finally, on the morning of November 20, 1887, we descended the last rocky step of the eastern escarpment onto the flat, sun-baked alluvial plain at the southern end of Lake Baringo (3,220 feet), and marched at 11 A.M. amidst booming salutes of welcome into Count Teleki's shady camp on the bank of the Guaso Tigerish, just outside the double stockade of the Wakwafi settlement of Njemps Mkubwa!

**[Illustration: VILLAGE OF NJEMPS MKUBWA NEAR LAKE BARINGO]**

Let me now go back and fill in from Count Teleki's journal and my own notes the complete narrative of the ascent of Mount Kenia, the botanical and geological features of the mountain, and the daily incidents of our march from Ndoro across Leikipia to Larabwal and Njemps.

First, as regards the ethnography of the northern Wakikuyu of Ndoro (6,380 feet), amongst whom we lived on friendly terms from September 19 to October 20: in physical appearance, language, and customs they agree closely with their southern kinsmen between Ngongo Bagas and Mathira, except that through constant intercourse and intermarriage with the neighbouring *Wandorobbo* hunters of the Kenia forests and the pastoral Masai of Leikipia they have adopted even more thoroughly the Masai style of dress, hair-dressing, and weapons. Instead of the poisoned bows and arrows which form the chief weapon of the southern Wakikuyu, the young warriors (*Anake*) of Ndoro carry almost universally the long, broad-bladed Masai spear, the oval painted buffalo-hide shield, and the double-edged sword (*ruhiu*), though for hunting elephants and buffaloes in the dense bamboo jungle of Mount Kenia they also use a peculiar heavy lance with a broad, razor-sharp iron head two feet long fitted into a short, thick shaft of solid wood weighted at the butt with an iron ferrule, which they drive into the beast from above as he passes beneath a tree or pitfalls!

---

&lt;!-- Page 371 --&gt;
*ASCENT OF MOUNT KENIA AND JOURNEY TO BARINGO* **371**

While Count Teleki was absent on his memorable six days' expedition up Mount Kenia (October 8 to 14), let us follow step by step from his own detailed notes the record of that arduous climb and the succession of vertical plant-zones on the western slope of the giant mountain.

On leaving the cultivated fields of Ndoro (6,380 feet) on the morning of October 8, the path led due east across a strip of bushy pasture and fern-clad hills into the lower primeval forest (*Subugo*) at an elevation of 6,750 feet. Here the forest consists of magnificent straight-stemmed timber trees rising to heights of a hundred and twenty to a hundred and sixty feet—chiefly the lofty East African cedar or juniper (*Juniperus procera*), whose fragrant reddish wood closely resembles that of the pencil-cedar, two species of yew-leaved *Podocarpus* (*P. elongata* and *P. Mannii*), tall wild olives (*Olea chrysophylla*), giant fig-trees, and glossy-leaved *Calodendrons*—bound together in the lower levels by thickets of wild coffee, tree-ferns, passion-vines, and stinging nettles, and carpeted underfoot with deep layers of black leaf-mould, mosses, and selaginellas.

Up to an altitude of 8,000 feet this noble timber forest continues unbroken along the steep rocky ridges between the deeply cut beds of the mountain brooks, which rush down in a continuous series of cascades over ledges of dark basaltic lava and coarse volcanic agglomerate. Throughout this zone the forest is uninhabited save by wandering families of *Wandorobbo* hunters, who set pitfalls and weighted spear-traps along the wide, muddy tunnels trodden through the undergrowth by the herds of elephants and buffaloes that ascend from the plains during the dry season to feed on the succulent young bamboo shoots and juicy forest herbage, and by troops of the magnificent long-haired black-and-white silk monkey (*Colobus guereza caudatus*), whose bushy white tail and flowing white mantle hang down like a snowy fleece as he leaps from bough to bough among the dark green junipers!

---

&lt;!-- Page 372 --&gt;
**372** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

At 8,100 feet the character of the vegetation changes with surprising suddenness: the mixed timber trees disappear almost entirely, and their place is taken by a dense, monotonous forest of gigantic hollow bamboos (*Arundinaria alpina*), rising like a wall of green lances thirty to fifty feet high and two to four inches in diameter! Scarcely a single flowering plant or bush can exist in the dim, chilly twilight beneath the overarching feathery plumes of this bamboo zone, which girdles the entire mountain between 8,100 and 10,000 feet; the ground is covered a foot deep with rotting yellow bamboo sheaths and slimy black mud, across which the elephants and buffaloes have trampled a labyrinth of miry ditches two feet deep, while every breeze shakes down a cold shower-bath of condensed mist-drops from the wet leaves above!

After passing the night of October 8 in a small dripping clearing in the midst of the bamboos at 8,850 feet (minimum temperature 43° Fahr.), Count Teleki continued the ascent on October 9 without the four Wakikuyu guides—who had bolted back to Ndoro at dawn in superstitious terror of the mountain demon *Ngai*—cutting his way straight up the western ridge by compass bearing. About 9,600 feet the bamboos began to grow shorter and less dense, giving room to picturesque groups of gnarled, moss-covered *Podocarpus* and broad-crowned *Hagenia abyssinica* (*kousso* trees), from whose twisted boughs hung grey beards of *Usnea barbata* three to four feet long.

At twelve o'clock noon on October 9, at an elevation of exactly 10,500 feet (3,200 metres), the upper edge of the forest and bamboo belt was reached, and before the climbers stretched the open, steeply rising Alpine moorland (*formation of Senecio and Lobelia*), bathed in rolling banks of cold grey mist and drizzle. Here the mountain slope forms a series of broad, boggy ridges radiating like giant buttresses from the foot of the central rocky peak and separated from each other by deep, U-shaped valleys worn smooth by ancient glaciers and traversed at the bottom by crystal-clear brooks flowing from the snow-fields above.

---

&lt;!-- Page 373 --&gt;
*ASCENT OF MOUNT KENIA AND JOURNEY TO BARINGO* **373**

Nothing in the whole vegetable kingdom could be more weird and unearthly than the aspect of this upper Alpine zone of Mount Kenia between 10,500 and 13,500 feet! Over the spongy, water-logged turf—formed of coarse tussock-grasses, green cushions of sphagnum moss, dwarf shrubby *Alchemillas*, and white and pink everlasting flowers (*Helichrysum*)—stand scattered by thousands the grotesque, charred-looking trunks of the arborescent groundsel (*Senecio Johnstoni*), rising to a height of fifteen or twenty feet like miniature cabbage-palms or giant candelabra, their bare woody stems wrapped in a thick protective sheath of dead withered leaves and crowned at the top of each forked branch with a dense rosette of large, fleshy green leaves and a huge bunch of yellow composite blossoms! Side by side with these tree-groundsels rise the stiff, monumental columns of two remarkable giant lobelias—one (*Lobelia Deckeni*) sending up a thick hollow spike of dark blue flowers nested in broad green bracts to a height of six or eight feet, while the other (a new species discovered by Count Teleki and named in his honour by Professor Schweinfurth *Lobelia Telekii*) shoots up a slender, furry pale-green obelisk ten to twelve feet high, densely clothed with long, drooping linear bracts that look from a distance like the silky hair of a green yak's tail!

**[Illustration: BAMBOO JUNGLE AND TREE-HEATHS ON MOUNT KENIA]**

Through this strange, prehistoric-looking landscape Count Teleki and his shivering Zanzibaris struggled upwards all the afternoon of October 9 and morning of October 10, sinking at every third step up to the knees in ice-cold black bog-mud between the grass tussocks, and pitched their highest bivouac on the afternoon of the 10th at an altitude of 13,100 feet under a huge overhanging block of phonolite near the head of the Guaso Narok valley. Here the last stunted *Senecio* trees furnished fuel for the night's camp-fire, round which the coast porters huddled closely wrapped in double woollen blankets while the thermometer dropped under a starry sky to 18.5° Fahr. (-7.5° C.)!

---

&lt;!-- Page 374 --&gt;
**374** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

Starting at dawn on October 11 from the bivouac at 13,100 feet with Qualla and six volunteers, Count Teleki climbed straight up the steep, frost-shattered ridge of reddish-grey rock and frozen screes bounding the south side of the upper valley. At 13,600 feet the last solitary specimens of *Senecio* and *Lobelia Telekii* were left behind, and only tiny tufts of brownish grass, dwarf mosses, and grey and orange crust-lichens clung to the crevices of the naked ice-polished rocks. At 13,800 feet the six Zanzibaris complained of violent dizziness, throbbing temples, and bleeding at the lips, and were ordered to wait in a sunny hollow of the rocks; at 14,200 feet Qualla's strength also gave out, and Count Teleki climbed on alone up the steep snow-beds and glazed rock-ledges of the western spur.

At 14,750 feet the limit of perpetual snow on the western face of Kenia was crossed, and after forty minutes' further stiff climbing with Alpine axe and nailed boots up a razor-edged arête of dark, glassy *kenyte* rock powdered with fresh hail and frozen snow, the Count reached at 11 A.M. the culminating point of the western rim of the ancient outer crater at an altitude determined by two boiling-point thermometers and aneroid as 15,355 feet (4,680 metres) above the sea!

Let me quote Count Teleki's own description of the grand Alpine panorama that met his eyes from this snowy pinnacle under the equator:

> 'Before me to the east the rock wall on which I stood fell away in a sheer precipice of five hundred feet into a circular cauldron or *cirque* nearly a mile in diameter, representing the ruined floor of the ancient volcanic crater, now completely filled by a vast, undulating field of blue-green glacier ice, seamed by wide transverse and longitudinal crevasses! From the further (eastern) side of this ice-filled basin rose with stupendous steepness the dark, snow-streaked central pyramid of Mount Kenia—not a hollow crater-cone like Kibo, but a solid, jagged tooth of black crystalline rock split at the summit into two sharp rocky horns of nearly equal height, rising fully three thousand feet above the glacier floor at their base to an elevation of between 18,400 and 19,000 feet! Down the vertical couloirs and ledges of this central rock-tooth descended several steep hanging glaciers and snow-slopes which fed the main ice-stream in the cauldron below, while from the western lip of the glacier basin issued the icy milky torrent of the Guaso Narok, tumbling down a series of frozen waterfalls into the valley by which I had ascended. Backwards to the west the eye ranged over the dark green ocean of the bamboo and timber forest clothing the foot-slopes of Kenia, across the sunny yellow plains of the Guaso Nyiro and Leikipia, to the long blue wall of the Aberdare range (*Sattima*), forty miles away!'

---

&lt;!-- Page 375 --&gt;
*ASCENT OF MOUNT KENIA AND JOURNEY TO BARINGO* **375**

After staying an hour on the windy crest at 15,355 feet—where the air temperature in the shade at noon was exactly 32° Fahr. (0° C.), and the boiling-point of water 184.6° Fahr. (84.8° C.)—taking photographs, compass bearings, and rock specimens, Count Teleki descended rapidly by his upward tracks, picking up Qualla and the six Zanzibaris on the way, and reached the bivouac at 13,100 feet at three o'clock in the afternoon. On October 12 and 13 the whole party descended through the Alpine moorland, bamboo jungle, and lower timber forest without mishap, arriving in our main camp at Ndoro on the afternoon of October 14, where the returning mountaineers were greeted with frantic cheers and firing of guns by their comrades!

Let us now follow in fuller detail our journey from Ndoro across the plateau of Leikipia to the valley of Larabwal and down the escarpment to Njemps on Lake Baringo (from October 20 to November 20, 1887).

When we marched out of our fortified camp at Ndoro on the morning of October 20, accompanied by our thirty hired Wakikuyu porters carrying the reserve bags of *wimbi* flour and beans, our course lay at first north-north-west along the gently sloping western base of Mount Kenia, just outside the dark green wall of the primeval forest (*Subugo*), across rolling grassy ridges intersected every two or three miles by rushing, ice-cold mountain streams. That first day we crossed the Guaso Narok, the Guaso Nairobi, and the Burguret, camping after a pleasant four hours' march beside a charming wooded bend of the Rongai river (6,240 feet). In the afternoon Count Teleki shot two fat zebras and a waterbuck half a mile below the camp, while I bagged six brace of large francolins (*Francolinus Schuettii*) and four brace of yellow-billed ducks along the stream.

---

&lt;!-- Page 376 --&gt;
**376** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

On October 21 we marched for four hours and a half due north across wide, breezy downs of waving yellow grass dotted with flat-topped acacias and clumps of tall *Juniperus procera*, crossing exactly on the Equator (0° 0' lat.) the crystal-clear, foaming torrent of the Nanyuki river (6,350 feet), which descends straight from the great western glacier of Mount Kenia and rushes over a bed of water-worn blocks of grey kenyte and basalt between shady banks of podocarpus, juniper, and wild olive trees! Half an hour beyond the Nanyuki we pitched our camp beside another cold glacier stream, the Likii (6,420 feet), from which we enjoyed a glorious unclouded view of the whole western face of Mount Kenia at sunset, its twin central rock-towers and hanging glaciers glowing like burnished copper above the dark green pedestal of forest!

On October 22 and 23 we curved round towards the north-west and west-north-west across uninhabited park-like steppes teeming with big game, camping on the 22nd by the clear brook of Sirimon (6,380 feet) and on the 23rd by the small marshy stream of Ngare Naju (6,010 feet). During these two days' marches we sighted no fewer than eleven rhinoceroses, four large herds of buffaloes, and hundreds of zebras, elands, and Jackson's hartebeests (*Alcelaphus Jacksoni*, distinguished from Coke's hartebeest by its much longer, high-set, lyre-shaped horns and brighter chestnut coat), of which the Count and I shot four rhinoceroses, three buffaloes, two elands, and five zebras to feed our hungry column of three hundred men!

**[Illustration: HEAD OF JACKSON'S HARTEBEEST (ALCELAPHUS JACKSONI)]**

On October 24 a march of four hours west-north-west down a gentle slope overgrown with grey-green sage-bush (*leleshwa*, *Tarchonanthus camphoratus*) and thorny whistling acacias brought us at 10.30 A.M. to the main channel of the upper Guaso Nyiro (5,650 feet), flowing northward in a clear, rapid stream thirty yards wide and two feet and a half deep over ledges of grey gneiss and basaltic boulders, shaded by magnificent flat-crowned *Acacia spirocarpa* and wild date-palms (*Phoenix reclinata*). Here our thirty hired Wakikuyu porters from Ndoro laid down their grain bags, received their promised payment of five coils of iron wire and ten strings of beads apiece, and set off at a trot on their homeward journey before evening, leaving us to transport our sixty extra loads forward by double stages with our own men!

---

&lt;!-- Page 377 --&gt;
*ASCENT OF MOUNT KENIA AND JOURNEY TO BARINGO* **377**

On October 25 and 26 we remained encamped on the left (western) bank of the Guaso Nyiro while eighty porters went ahead half a day's march to deposit the surplus grain-loads at the next water-hole and return for their regular bales. While waiting by the river we had splendid sport with the herds of waterbuck (*Cobus ellipsiprymnus*), impala, zebra, and buffalo that came down morning and evening to drink at the fords, Count Teleki killing two huge old bull buffaloes, a female rhinoceros, and three waterbucks, while I caught several dozen fine barbel weighing up to four pounds apiece with hook and line in the deep rocky pools of the stream.

On October 27 and 28 we ascended steadily west-north-west from the valley of the Guaso Nyiro onto the broad, undulating tableland of Leikipia (6,100 to 6,450 feet), marching over rich grassy downs interspersed with groves of camphor-scented *leleshwa* bush, wild olive, and thorny acacia, and camping beside clear water-holes in the rocky beds of the Ngare Kidongoi and Guaso Narok (of Leikipia)—the latter a deep, swift highland river flowing north-eastward from the northern end of the Aberdare range (*Sattima*) to join the Guaso Nyiro.

On October 29 and 30 our route skirted the northern edge of the great reed-grown swamp of Marmanet or Pesi (6,350 feet), formed by the spreading out of the waters of the Guaso Narok over a flat basin five miles long by two wide before entering its rocky lower gorge. Around the marshy margins of this vast lagoon we beheld a spectacle of wild animal life that recalled the descriptions of South Africa in the days of Gordon Cumming and Baldwin: three distinct herds of buffaloes numbering at least four hundred to six hundred head apiece lay wallowing like black boulders in the shallow mud-pools or grazed along the green belt of turf bordering the reeds; troops of twenty and thirty elands, zebras, and waterbucks trotted up and down the slopes; flocks of thousands of wild ducks, Egyptian geese, sacred ibises, marabou storks, and crowned cranes rose with a roar like thunder from the water-channels; while in the course of two hours' walk along the swamp edge we counted nine rhinoceroses and the fresh night-tracks of a huge herd of elephants!

---

&lt;!-- Page 378 --&gt;
**378** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

Here by the Pesi swamp on the afternoon of October 29 Count Teleki had one of the narrowest escapes of our whole journey while following up a wounded buffalo bull into a patch of tall reeds and *leleshwa* scrub: the infuriated monster lay in wait beside his own bloody track until the Count was within five paces, and then charged out like a whirlwind with lowered horns and blood-shot eyes! A heavy eight-bore bullet fired point-blank into his forehead failed to penetrate the massive convex bosses of the horns, merely bringing the bull to his knees for a second; before the Count could snatch his second rifle from Qualla, the buffalo was upon him, knocking him flat on his back in the mud and driving one horn deep into the tough stock of the heavy rifle which he held crosswise as a shield over his chest! At that critical instant Qualla and Maktubu fired both barrels simultaneously into the brute's shoulder at two yards' distance, rolling him stone-dead right across the Count's legs, who happily escaped with a few severe bruises and a wrenched thumb!

On October 31 and November 1 we marched north-westward across the highest western swells of the Leikipia plateau (6,600 to 6,850 feet), approaching the picturesque rugged range of granitic and gneiss hills known as the Ongalea mountains, which rise eight hundred feet above the rolling tableland near the western edge of the Great Rift escarpment. As our caravan was filing through a rocky pass at the southern foot of the Ongalea peaks on the morning of November 1, our vanguard suddenly sighted a magnificent solitary bull elephant standing fanning his huge ears beneath a group of flat-topped mimosa trees three hundred yards to leeward of the path! Ordering the column to halt and sit down silently in the grass, Count Teleki crept down a dry gully with Qualla and his double four-bore rifle to within forty paces of the giant beast, and planted a four-ounce hardened conical ball right in the hollow between eye and ear! With a shrill trumpet that echoed from cliff to cliff across the valley, the colossus reared upright on his hind legs, spun half round, and crashed dead to the earth like a falling tower! His massive, symmetrical tusks, when chopped out after four hours' hard work with axes, measured seven feet four inches and seven feet two inches in length, and weighed together exactly one hundred and seventy-two pounds of faultless white ivory!

**[Illustration: COUNT TELEKI'S BIG BULL ELEPHANT NEAR THE ONGALEA HILLS]**

---

&lt;!-- Page 379 --&gt;
*ASCENT OF MOUNT KENIA AND JOURNEY TO BARINGO* **379**

On November 2 and 3 we crossed the broken, rocky spurs to the west of the Ongalea hills (6,500 feet) and began our descent over the upper volcanic terraces of the western Leikipia escarpment, camping on November 4 in the green, sheltered highland valley of Larabwal (5,820 feet), watered by a chain of clear rocky pools and shaded by fine groves of wild olive, juniper, and mimosa.

As already explained, it was here at Larabwal on the morning of November 5 that Count Teleki went ahead down the precipitous escarpment to Njemps with Jumbe Kimemeta and a hundred picked porters to establish our permanent headquarters near Lake Baringo, while I remained encamped at Larabwal for twelve days (November 5 to 16) with one hundred and seventy men, the sick list, the cattle herd, and sixty extra loads of grain and ivory until the Count's porters returned from Njemps to help carry the surplus baggage down into the Rift Valley!

During those twelve peaceful days in the lonely mountain glen of Larabwal my time was fully occupied from morning till night with astronomical and meteorological observations, plotting our route-maps from Taveta to Kenia and Leikipia, looking after the sores and ailments of the convalescent porters, and hunting big game on the surrounding hills and grassy flats to supply my hundred and seventy men with meat and prepare sixty loads of sun-dried *charqui* for our stay in the barren basin of Baringo.

Let me quote a few characteristic entries from my hunting journal at Larabwal:

> '*November 7.*—Set out at 6 A.M. with Schaedler and ten men down the grassy valley towards the south-west, where the Larabwal brook enters a narrow rocky gorge bordered by dense thickets of *leleshwa* and wait-a-bit thorn. Half a mile below camp we came upon a herd of eighty buffaloes grazing slowly back from the water towards the shady hillside. Stalking upwind behind a line of ant-hills to within eighty yards of the rearmost animals, I dropped a heavy old bull in his tracks with a .500 Express solid bullet through the neck-vertebrae, and as the herd halted in momentary confusion to look back at their fallen leader, bowled over a fat cow with my left barrel through the lungs! Before noon forty men had cut up both carcasses and carried every scrap of meat and marrow-bones into camp, where long scaffolds of green poles were erected over slow wood-fires to smoke and dry the long strips of flesh.'

---

&lt;!-- Page 380 --&gt;
**380** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

> '*November 9.*—While returning towards camp at five o'clock this afternoon with four Somal attendants after shooting two elands and a waterbuck on the plateau to the north, we suddenly walked within twenty-five paces of two huge rhinoceroses—a cow and a nearly full-grown calf—standing asleep in the shadow of a thick *Capparis* bush! Awakened by the click of my rifle-lock, both animals spun round with ear-splitting snorts like a steam-engine blowing off steam, and came charging straight down the narrow game-path at us! Drop on one knee, I fired my eight-bore right into the old cow's chest at ten yards' distance, turning her sharp off into the scrub with a broken shoulder, while my left barrel caught the younger beast behind the ear and stretched him lifeless within four paces of the bush behind which my men had dived! Following up the blood-spoor of the cow for three hundred yards through the thorns, we found her standing swaying from side to side in a small clearing, where a second bullet in the neck finished her.'

> '*November 12.*—Last night about eleven o'clock two lions crept up in the pitch darkness against the wind to the leeward side of our cattle-boma, and with a tremendous bound leaped right onto the top of the eight-foot thorn hedge! Terrified by the deep, thunderous roars of the beasts and the smell of blood, our forty bullocks stampeded wildly against the opposite side of the kraal fence, breaking down the stout stakes and dashing out into the main camp over the sleeping porters! Instantly the whole camp was in an uproar: two hundred men leaped up yelling and firing their rifles blindly into the air, while I ran out of my tent with a lantern and my double Express just in time to see a huge maned lion spring back across the broken thorn hedge with a full-grown sheep in his jaws! Half a dozen bullets sent after him in the dark missed him clean, and next morning we found only the head and trotters of the sheep two hundred yards down the ravine.'

On November 15 Qualla and Maktubu marched into Larabwal camp at the head of ninety rested Zanzibaris sent up by Count Teleki from Njemps, bringing a welcome budget of news from the valley below: the Count had reached Njemps safely on November 7 after three hard days' scramble down the rocky escarpment, had been most cordially received by the Wakwafi elders of Njemps Mkubwa and Njemps Ndogo, and had pitched a roomy, shady camp on the bank of the Guaso Tigerish river, where he was impatiently awaiting our arrival before starting on a fortnight's hunting and exploring trip round the shores of Lake Baringo!

---

&lt;!-- Page 381 --&gt;
*ASCENT OF MOUNT KENIA AND JOURNEY TO BARINGO* **381**

Breaking up our pleasant highland camp at Larabwal at daybreak on November 17, we marched westward with our reunited column of two hundred and sixty men—not a single bale or bag of flour having been left behind!—to the edge of the upper escarpment, where a magnificent bird's-eye view burst upon us over the whole central basin of the Great Rift Valley! Three thousand feet below our feet lay stretched the flat, bush-clad floor of the meridional trough, twenty-five miles wide, shut in on the further (western) side by the dark, precipitous wall of the Kamasia range (7,000 to 8,000 feet), beyond which peeped the still loftier blue crest of the Elgeyo escarpment (9,000 feet). Right in the middle of the valley floor gleamed the oblong, island-studded sheet of Lake Baringo, some fourteen miles long by seven wide, bordered at its southern end by the green gallery woods of the Guaso Tigerish and Guaso Molo rivers, while to the north of the lake rose a chaotic wilderness of dark volcanic cones, jagged lava crags, and barren red hills stretching away towards the unknown regions of Karamojo and Samburu!

**[Illustration: LAKE BARINGO AND THE KAMASIA ESCARPMENT FROM THE LEIKIPIA RIM]**

Our descent down this stupendous volcanic staircase during November 17, 18, and 19 was one of the roughest pieces of marching in our whole expedition. The western face of the Leikipia plateau drops from 6,000 feet to 3,200 feet in three giant step-faults or terraces of dark grey trachyte and cellular black basalt, separated by narrow, boulder-strewn valleys down which rush intermittent mountain torrents during the rains. Along the steep, zig-zag game-tracks leading down the cliff walls our heavily laden porters had to lower the iron boat-sections, ammunition boxes, and big elephant tusks from ledge to ledge by hand, while the horses, donkeys, and bullocks slid on their haunches over rolling stones and sharp lava blocks under a blazing sun that sent the thermometer up to 96° Fahr. in the shade by midday!

After camping on November 17 by the scanty rock-pools of Morijo or Maza (4,650 feet), and on November 18 by the romantic rocky gorge of the Mukutan brook (3,850 feet)—where the stream plunges through a narrow cleft in the basalt wall into a series of deep green rock-basins swarming with small silvery fish—we descended the last stony step of the escarpment on the morning of November 20 onto the level alluvial plain of Njemps (3,220 feet), and marched at eleven o'clock into Count Teleki's camp beside the Guaso Tigerish!

---

&lt;!-- Page 382 --&gt;
**382** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

Here at Njemps we found ourselves once more at a well-known landmark of East African exploration, where Joseph Thomson had rested four years before (in November 1883) on his memorable journey to Mount Elgon and the Victoria Nyanza, and where every year or two large Swahili trading caravans from Pangani and Mombasa establish their base camp before pushing northward to barter iron wire and beads for ivory in Samburu, Suk, and Turkanaland.

The district of Njemps—called *En-jemusi* by the natives themselves—consists of a flat alluvial plain watered by two perennial streams descending from the Mau and Kamasia escarpments: the Guaso Tigerish (or Perkerra), which flows right past the two fortified villages of Njemps Mkubwa ('Great Njemps') and Njemps Ndogo ('Little Njemps') and dries up or loses itself in a swamp a couple of miles before reaching Lake Baringo during the dry season, and the Guaso Molo, a larger, sluggish stream five miles further east which flows right into the southern end of the lake through a reedy delta swarming with hippopotami, crocodiles, and water-fowl.

The inhabitants of the two villages of Njemps—numbering together barely fifteen hundred to two thousand souls—are a peaceful, industrious colony of agricultural Wakwafi (*Il Oogolala*), the descendants of pastoral Masai families from Leikipia and Naivasha who lost all their cattle during the great civil wars a generation ago and took refuge in this fertile plain at the foot of the Kamasia hills. Surrounded on all sides by fierce predatory neighbours—the Masai of Naivasha on the south, the Wakamasia and Wasuk on the west and north-west, and the wandering Turkana and Samburu on the north—the people of Njemps have enclosed each of their two villages in a strong double circular stockade of heavy acacia trunks and thorny brushwood ten feet high, pierced by only two narrow, low gateways that are barricaded every evening at sunset with stout beams and thorn bushes. Inside this cramped ring stand huddled closely together a hundred and fifty to two hundred small conical huts thatched with dry grass, without a single yard of garden or cattle-pen between them, so that the narrow winding lanes are ankle-deep in dust and sweepings and swarm with lean pariah dogs, rats, and fleas!

**[Illustration: DOUBLE STOCKADE AND GATEWAY OF NJEMPS MKUBWA]**

---

&lt;!-- Page 383 --&gt;
*ASCENT OF MOUNT KENIA AND JOURNEY TO BARINGO* **383**

As they possess scarcely a score of lean goats and sheep and not a single head of cattle, the Wakwafi of Njemps subsist almost entirely upon agriculture, fishing, and hunting with traps and pitfalls. With admirable ingenuity and industry they have constructed a dam of felled tree-trunks, boulders, and brushwood across the bed of the Guaso Tigerish a mile above Njemps Mkubwa, from which a regular network of artificial irrigation canals and ditches leads the muddy, fertilizing water over hundreds of acres of flat fields divided into small square beds by low earthen ridges! Whenever the grain is ripe or the ground needs watering, the sluices in the main canal are opened for a few hours by the village elders in rotation, flooding each family plot in turn and enabling the inhabitants in normal years to raise bountiful crops of small red millet (*wimbi*, *Eleusine coracana*), sorghum (*mtama*), sweet melons, pumpkins, beans, and tobacco despite the scorching heat and scanty rainfall of the Baringo depression!

Unfortunately for us, however, a couple of months before our arrival an unprecedented flood following a cloud-burst in the Kamasia mountains had swept away the main irrigation weir across the Guaso Tigerish and drowned half the standing crops in mud and sand, so that instead of finding abundant grain at Njemps as we had hoped, the inhabitants themselves were on the verge of famine and begged eagerly around our camp for scraps of our dried buffalo-meat and handfuls of our Kikuyu flour!

How fervently we congratulated ourselves and thanked Count Teleki's foresight that we had brought down with us from Ndoro and Larabwal nearly a hundred and fifty loads of dry grain, beans, and smoked meat, which placed us beyond all danger of starvation for the next six weeks! Still, before we could venture to launch out with nearly three hundred men into the totally unknown, arid wilderness stretching for hundreds of miles to the north of Lake Baringo towards our great goal—the mysterious inland lakes of Samburuland—it was absolutely imperative to procure at least fifty or sixty loads more of grain from the mountain tribes of Kamasia, Elgeyo, or Marakwet to the west of the Rift Valley!

---

&lt;!-- Page 384 --&gt;
**384** *DISCOVERY OF LAKES RUDOLF AND STEFANIE*

Accordingly, immediately after my arrival at Njemps on November 20, it was decided that Jumbe Kimemeta and Maktubu should set out on November 22 with eighty porters and a stock of beads, iron wire, and cowrie shells on a fortnight's trading expedition across the Kamasia range into the fertile valley of the Kerio river to purchase *mtama* and *wimbi* grain from the agricultural tribes of Elgeyo and Marakwet, while I remained in charge of the main camp at Njemps to complete our maps and astronomical observations and overhaul the loads, and Count Teleki made a two weeks' hunting and exploring tour round the western and northern shores of Lake Baringo (from November 23 to December 6)!

During the fortnight following November 22 my life in the shady camp under the tall acacias beside the Guaso Tigerish passed quietly and busily enough, though the climate of the deep Baringo trough (3,220 feet) proved vastly hotter, drier, and more trying than the bracing mountain air of Kikuyu and Leikipia: every afternoon the thermometer in the shade of my double tent rose to 95° or 98° Fahr., accompanied by furious dusty whirlwinds from the north-east that swept pillars of red sand high into the sky across the parched plain, while at night swarms of vicious mosquitoes from the neighbouring irrigation ditches and the Molo swamp made sleep impossible without closely tucked mosquito-nets!

Every two or three days nimble runners sent back by Count Teleki arrived in camp laden with fat haunches of waterbuck, impala, zebra, and hippopotamus meat, or bringing news of his progress along the rocky western shore of Lake Baringo and around the northern bays of the lake. Let me summarize from the Count's diary the geographical results and sporting incidents of this interesting excursion round Lake Baringo.

Marching northward from Njemps Mkubwa on November 23 across six miles of flat, cracked alluvial clay thinly overgrown with thorny mimosa scrub and tall reeds, the Count reached the marshy southern shore of Lake Baringo (3,190 feet above the sea) near the mouth of the Guaso Molo, where hundreds of hippopotami lay sunning their pink-and-brown backs on the mud-banks, while huge crocodiles twelve to fifteen feet long glided noiselessly into the muddy, pea-green water at the approach of the party. Unlike Lakes Naivasha, Elmenteita, and Nakuro further south in the Rift Valley, the water of Lake Baringo is perfectly fresh and drinkable, though warm and slightly turbid near the mouths of the southern rivers; as the lake has no visible surface outlet whatever to the north, and receives perennial streams at its southern end (the Guaso Molo, Guaso Arabel, and Ol Arabel), its freshness is doubtless explained by a subterranean drainage escape through the porous, fissured basaltic lavas at its northern extremity!

---

&lt;!-- Page 385 --&gt;
*ASCENT OF MOUNT KENIA AND JOURNEY TO BARINGO* **385**

From the southern shore Count Teleki marched along the rugged, rocky western side of the lake, where steep spurs of dark trachytic and basaltic lava descend from the foot-hills of Kamasia right down to the water's edge, indented by charming little crescent-shaped bays and rocky headlands. Off the middle of the lake rises a picturesque group of five volcanic islands, the largest of which—called *Ol Kokwa*—is about two miles and a half long and rises four hundred feet above the water, representing the broken rim of an ancient tuff-crater containing boiling hot sulphur springs on its inner shore and inhabited by a few poor families of Wakwafi fishermen and goat-herds who paddle backwards and forwards to the mainland on rude, cranky rafts (*oboro*) bound together out of six or eight logs of the Cork-like *ambatch* wood (*Herminiera elaphroxylon*)!

**[Illustration: AMBATCH CANOE ON LAKE BARINGO]**

After rounding the rocky northern shore of the lake—where the water was deep, crystal-clear, and swarming with fish and crocodiles, while the bare red lava hills around were visited only by wandering families of Suk herdsmen and hunters—Count Teleki returned along the eastern shore past the reedy mouth of the Ol Arabel river, reaching our main camp at Njemps on the afternoon of December 6 after bagging during his fourteen days' trip four hippopotami, three rhinoceroses, two buffaloes, fourteen waterbucks, eleven impalas, eight Grant's gazelles, and ten zebras!

On the following day (December 7, 1887) Jumbe Kimemeta and Maktubu also marched back into camp from their trading expedition to Kamasia and Elgeyo, bringing sixty-five full loads of *mtama* and *wimbi* grain, so that all was now ready for our long-prepared advance northward into the unknown lands where lay the hidden lakes of Rudolf and Stefanie!

#### END OF THE FIRST VOLUME

### [FOLD-OUT MAPS AT END OF VOLUME I]

#### Main Map:
**A MAP OF THE REGIONS EXPLORED BY COUNT SAMUEL TELEKI'S EXPEDITION TO EAST AFRICA, 1887–88.**
*Plotted and drawn by Lieutenant Ludwig von Höhnel.*
Scale 1 : 1,350,000 (approximately 21.3 English miles to 1 inch).
Altitudes in English Feet. Count Teleki's Route shown in red.
London: Longmans & Co. Stanford's Geogl. Estabt.

- **Southern Sheet Coverage (Vol. I)**: Extends from 7° 0' S. lat. (Dar-es-Salaam, Bagamoyo, Zanzibar, Pangani) northward through Usambara, Pare, Taveta, Mount Kilimanjaro (Kibo 19,680 ft., Kimawenzi 17,290 ft.), Useri, Nyiri Plain, Donyo Erok el Matumbato (6,560 ft.), Kapotei, Donyo Lamuyu / Ngongo Bagas (6,100 ft.), Kikuyuland, Aberdare Range (Donyo Sattima), Mount Kenia (18,400–19,000 ft.; Count Teleki's highest point 15,355 ft.), Guaso Nyiro, Leikipia Plateau, Larabwal, and Njemps to Lake Baringo (3,190 ft.) in 0° 40' N. lat.

#### Inset Maps on the Same Sheet:
1. **Geological Sketch Map of the Eastern Portion of Equatorial Africa** (after Prof. F. Toula, based on Lieut. von Höhnel's observations and rock collections):
   - Shows distribution of Ancient Crystalline Rocks (Gneiss, Granite, Crystalline Schists), Carbonic Sandstones and Shales of the Coast Zone, Jurassic Limestones, and Young Volcanic Formations (Basalts, Trachytes, Phonolites, Kenyte, Tuffs) along the East African Rift Valley (*Graben*) and the volcanic massifs of Kilimanjaro, Meru, Lamuyu, Sattima, and Kenia.
2. **Sketch Map Showing the Density of Population in the Explored Regions**:
   - Distinguishes Uninhabited Steppe and Primeval Forest, Thinly Peopled Nomad Pastures (Masai, Samburu, Turkana, Rendile), Moderately Peopled Districts, and Densely Peopled Agricultural Highlands (Bondei, Usambara, Chaga, Taveta, Useri, Kikuyuland, Njemps, Reshiat).
3. **Ethnographical Sketch Map of the Explored Regions**:
   - Shows territorial boundaries of Bantu Tribes (Waseguha, Wabondei, Wasambara, Wapare, Wachaga, Wataveta, Wakamba, Wakikuyu, Wambere), Nilotic / Hamitic Pastoral and Mixed Tribes (Masai, Wakwafi, Wandorobbo, Wakamasia, Wasuk, Turkana, Karamojong, Donyiro), and Cushitic / Eastern Hamitic Tribes (Somal, Galla / Borana, Rendile, Reshiat, Marle, Amarr, Buma).
